CAT-VARC-P01-001 · Knowledge and belief · passage Medium · 439 words · 4 questions

A familiar picture of the responsible thinker holds that she believes nothing on another's say-so until she has satisfied herself that the speaker is reliable. On this view, testimony is a second-class source of knowledge, legitimate only when it can be cashed out in the currency of one's own perception and reasoning: I may believe the timetable because I have found timetables accurate before, and I may believe my doctor because I have, or could obtain, evidence of her competence. The picture has the appeal of self-reliance. It seems to keep the knower in charge of what she knows.

The difficulty is that almost nobody could meet its demands, and the few who tried would know very little. Consider how much of what an ordinary adult believes was simply told to her: the shape of the continents, the date of her own birth, the existence of cells, the fact that a city she has never visited lies where the maps place it. To check the reliability of these sources she would have to rely on further sources, whose reliability would in turn need checking. The independent track record the picture asks for is itself largely assembled from testimony. A child who refused to believe her teachers until she had verified their credentials would never acquire the language in which credentials are described.

It does not follow that we may believe whatever we are told. The better view is that a hearer is entitled to accept what she is told unless she has some reason for doubt: a speaker with an evident motive to deceive, a claim that collides with what she already has good grounds to believe, a manner that suggests guesswork rather than knowledge. On this view the burden is reversed. Trust is the default, and it is suspicion, not acceptance, that must be earned by evidence. This is not credulity, because the default is sensitive to defeaters; the hearer who ignores a glaring sign of deceit is not exercising her entitlement but neglecting it.

Defenders of the stricter picture are right about one thing. A default of trust is only as good as the hearer's alertness to the signals that should override it, and that alertness is unevenly distributed and easily exploited. The con artist's whole craft consists in suppressing the cues that would trigger doubt. But this shows that we need to cultivate sensitivity to defeaters, not that we must abandon trust as a starting point. A community in which everyone demanded proof before belief would not be more rational than ours; it would merely be poorer in what it could collectively know.

CAT-VARC-P01-001-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)showing that testimony is as reliable as perception and reasoning, so that what we are told should be accepted unless it is proved false.
  2. (B)defending a defeasible default of trust in what others tell us against the demand that every source be verified before it is believed.
  3. (C)explaining how con artists exploit the trust that ordinary hearers extend to speakers, and how hearers might learn to resist them.
  4. (D)defending the self-reliant thinker who believes nothing on another's say-so against the charge that such a thinker would know very little.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 states the picture that testimony must be verified, paragraph 2 argues that its demands cannot be met, paragraph 3 offers a 'better view' on which trust is the default unless there is reason for doubt, and paragraph 4 concedes a weakness while keeping trust as 'a starting point'. The passage as a whole defends the defeasible default against the verification demand.

Why not (A): The passage never ranks testimony's reliability against perception, and the default is overridden by any 'reason for doubt' (paragraph 3), not only by proof of falsehood.

Why not (C): The con artist appears in one sentence of paragraph 4 to illustrate the concession; the option takes a passing illustration for the purpose of the whole passage.

Why not (D): This reverses the argument: paragraph 2 makes exactly that charge against the self-reliant picture, and the author endorses it.

Common misconception: Choosing the con artist detail, or overstating the author's view into accepting anything not proved false.

Difficulty: One wrong option uses the passage's own language about reliability and acceptance; the correct answer requires seeing the critique and the alternative together. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-001-Q2 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The example of the child who refuses to believe her teachers (paragraph 2) serves mainly to
  1. (A)suggest that children are more credulous than adults and therefore acquire false beliefs more readily than adults do.
  2. (B)argue that schools should place less weight on the formal qualifications of the teachers they employ.
  3. (C)give an instance of testimony that a hearer ought to reject because the speaker has an evident motive to deceive.
  4. (D)show that the checking the stricter picture demands would itself depend on having first accepted what one was told.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The sentence before the example says the 'independent track record the picture asks for is itself largely assembled from testimony'. The child could not verify credentials without the language she learns by trusting her teachers, so verification presupposes prior trust.

Why not (A): The child in the example is not credulous at all; she refuses belief, and the passage says nothing about children acquiring false beliefs.

Why not (B): No recommendation about schools or hiring is made; the example concerns how anyone could come to understand talk of credentials.

Why not (C): Motives to deceive appear only in paragraph 3 as defeaters; the teachers in paragraph 2 are not presented as deceivers.

Common misconception: Reading the example as a remark about children or schools rather than as a point about the regress of checking.

Difficulty: The function is signalled by the sentence immediately before the example. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-001-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
A traveller asks a stranger for directions to the railway station and follows them, although she has noticed the stranger winking at his companions as if playing a prank. On the view the author defends, the traveller is best described as
  1. (A)acting reasonably, because trust is the default and the stranger had given her no proof that his directions were false.
  2. (B)acting as the stricter picture requires, since she ought first to have checked the stranger's record of giving accurate directions.
  3. (C)failing to use her entitlement to trust properly, because a plain sign of deceit should have overridden the default.
  4. (D)acting unreasonably only if the directions turn out to be wrong, since the outcome shows whether her trust was warranted.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says the default of trust 'is sensitive to defeaters' and that 'the hearer who ignores a glaring sign of deceit is not exercising her entitlement but neglecting it'. The wink is such a sign, so the traveller neglects rather than exercises her entitlement.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says the default yields to 'some reason for doubt', and the wink is such a reason; proof of falsehood is not required to override trust.

Why not (B): The author rejects the demand for a prior track record in paragraph 2; her fault on the author's view is ignoring a defeater, not failing to verify.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 locates the fault in ignoring 'a glaring sign of deceit', which is a fault whatever the outcome; the passage never makes rationality depend on results.

Common misconception: Treating the default of trust as unconditional, or judging the traveller by how things turn out.

Difficulty: Requires applying the defeater clause to a new case and resisting a wrong option that quotes the default. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-001-Q4 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred from paragraph 4 that the author locates the main weakness of a default of trust in
  1. (A)the uneven and exploitable ability of hearers to notice the signals that ought to override it.
  2. (B)its tendency to make a community poorer in what that community can collectively know.
  3. (C)the lack of any principled way to tell such trust apart from mere credulity in a hearer.
  4. (D)the burden it places on every speaker to prove her honesty before anyone believes her.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 concedes that the default 'is only as good as the hearer's alertness to the signals that should override it', and that this alertness 'is unevenly distributed and easily exploited', as the con artist shows. That is the weakness the author grants.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 attributes this effect to a community that 'demanded proof before belief', that is, to the stricter picture, not to the default of trust.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 supplies that principle: the default 'is not credulity, because the default is sensitive to defeaters'.

Why not (D): This reverses the view: under the default 'the burden is reversed' and it is suspicion, not acceptance, that must be earned (paragraph 3).

Common misconception: Attributing to the default the cost the author assigns to the stricter picture.

Difficulty: Two wrong options reuse phrases from paragraphs 3 and 4 but attach them to the wrong view. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-002 · Knowledge and belief · passage Medium · 437 words · 4 questions

The sceptic's argument has an irritating simplicity. You claim to know that you are sitting in a room reading. But if you were dreaming vividly, or if your experiences were being produced by some elaborate deception, everything would seem exactly as it now does. Since you cannot tell from the inside that you are not in such a situation, you cannot rule it out; and if you cannot rule it out, the sceptic concludes, you do not know that you are reading. The argument generalises to almost everything, and many careful attempts to refute it have the air of a lawyer winning on a technicality rather than of anyone actually seeing the truth.

One response is to concede the argument and shrug: perhaps we know nothing, but we must act as if we did, and so nothing of practical importance changes. This concession is too generous. It treats the sceptic's standard, that knowing requires excluding every alternative one cannot distinguish from the actual situation, as if it were simply the correct standard, which we happen to fall short of. Yet we nowhere apply that standard outside the philosophy seminar. A birdwatcher who reports a heron on the riverbank is not asked whether she has excluded the possibility of a mechanical decoy indistinguishable at that distance; she is asked whether she looked carefully, knows her birds and had a clear view. If a park ranger has just announced that decoys have been placed along that stretch of river, the question changes, and it is right that it should.

What this suggests is that the alternatives a knower must exclude are fixed by something other than bare conceivability: by what is live in the circumstances, by what a reasonable person has some ground to take seriously. The sceptic does not discover a hidden flaw in our ordinary claims; he changes the subject by importing a standard that no ordinary claim was ever meant to satisfy. This is why his argument feels at once unanswerable and beside the point.

None of this makes scepticism worthless. Its exaggerated standard has the merit of exposing how much of our confidence rests on background assumptions we rarely state, and in particular circumstances, the decoy case among them, those assumptions deserve scrutiny. The error lies in moving from the observation that every assumption could in principle be questioned to the conclusion that every assumption must be questioned before anything is known. The first is a useful reminder; the second would make knowledge impossible by definition, and a definition that guarantees its own emptiness is a poor account of what we were trying to describe.

CAT-VARC-P01-002-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the birdwatcher would properly be asked whether she had ruled out a mechanical decoy if
  1. (A)someone in a position to know had just announced that decoys were placed along that part of the river.
  2. (B)she could not tell from the inside whether she was dreaming the scene she reported to others.
  3. (C)a philosopher pointed out that a decoy indistinguishable at that distance is perfectly conceivable.
  4. (D)her view of the riverbank had been partly blocked by trees or by the light on the water.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says the birdwatcher is not ordinarily asked about decoys, but 'If a park ranger has just announced that decoys have been placed along that stretch of river, the question changes'.

Why not (B): This is the sceptic's general worry from paragraph 1, which the author says ordinary practice does not require anyone to answer.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the relevant alternatives are fixed by 'something other than bare conceivability', so pointing out a mere possibility does not change the question.

Why not (D): A poor view bears on the ordinary question of whether she 'had a clear view'; the passage ties the decoy question only to the ranger's announcement.

Common misconception: Confusing the ordinary checks (care, expertise, clear view) with the special circumstance that makes the decoy relevant.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-002-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In paragraph 3, the author says the sceptic 'changes the subject'. This most nearly means that the sceptic
  1. (A)introduces a new topic, dreaming, that has no bearing on whether someone is really sitting in a room reading.
  2. (B)uncovers a hidden flaw in ordinary claims to know that the people making those claims had failed to notice.
  3. (C)replaces the standard that governs ordinary claims to know with a far stricter one and then judges those claims by it.
  4. (D)abandons the question of what we know and turns instead to the practical question of how we ought to act.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The full sentence reads that the sceptic 'changes the subject by importing a standard that no ordinary claim was ever meant to satisfy'. The subject changes because the question being answered is no longer the ordinary one about knowledge but one set by a different, stricter standard.

Why not (A): This reads the phrase literally; dreaming bears directly on the sceptic's challenge, and the author's complaint is about the standard, not the topic.

Why not (B): The same sentence denies this: the sceptic 'does not discover a hidden flaw in our ordinary claims'.

Why not (D): Turning to how we must act is the 'shrug' response of paragraph 2, made by those who concede the argument, not the sceptic's own move.

Common misconception: Reading 'changes the subject' as a change of topic rather than a change of standard.

Difficulty: The literal sense of the idiom is a tempting wrong option; the correct answer needs the rest of the sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-002-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
The author's attitude to the response that would 'concede the argument and shrug' (paragraph 2) is best described as
  1. (A)approving, because the response rightly sees that the sceptic's conclusion leaves practical life entirely untouched.
  2. (B)critical, because the response grants the sceptic's standard a correctness that it has never been shown to have.
  3. (C)dismissive, because the response overlooks the real possibility that we are in fact dreaming at this moment.
  4. (D)ambivalent, because the response is generous to the sceptic in one respect but unfair to him in another.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says 'This concession is too generous' and explains that it treats the sceptic's standard 'as if it were simply the correct standard, which we happen to fall short of'. The author then argues that the standard is not the one we actually apply.

Why not (A): The author calls the concession 'too generous' (paragraph 2), which is a criticism, and never endorses the claim about practical life.

Why not (C): The author's complaint is the opposite: the response takes the sceptic too seriously, not too lightly, and the author never treats dreaming as a live possibility.

Why not (D): The passage finds the response too generous and identifies no respect in which it is unfair to the sceptic.

Common misconception: Taking the author's agreement that scepticism has some value (paragraph 4) as approval of the shrug.

Difficulty: Tone must be read from the reason the author gives, not from the word 'concede'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-002-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's account of which alternatives a knower must exclude?
  1. (A)Experienced birdwatchers misidentify herons far less often than beginners do, even when they view the birds from a considerable distance in poor light.
  2. (B)Many trained philosophers find the sceptic's argument very hard to refute, and some of them regard every refutation proposed so far as unsuccessful.
  3. (C)On rivers where decoys are known to be common, careful observers routinely check for them before they report a sighting of any wading bird.
  4. (D)In everyday conversation, people readily withdraw a claim to know as soon as an unlikely alternative is merely mentioned, even with no reason to think it obtains.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The author claims that the alternatives to be excluded are fixed by 'something other than bare conceivability', namely what is live in the circumstances, and supports this by saying 'we nowhere apply that standard outside the philosophy seminar'. If ordinary speakers in fact abandon knowledge claims whenever a mere possibility is raised, then the sceptic's standard is applied in ordinary practice, and the author's main evidence fails.

Why not (A): This concerns ordinary checks such as knowing one's birds, which the author already accepts; it says nothing about what fixes the relevant alternatives.

Why not (B): The author grants this in paragraph 1 ('the air of a lawyer winning on a technicality') and argues that it does not show the sceptic is right.

Why not (C): This fits the author's account: decoys become a live alternative in those circumstances, exactly as in the ranger case of paragraph 2.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about philosophers' difficulty, which the author has already conceded and answered.

Difficulty: Requires identifying that the author's account rests on a claim about ordinary practice and finding the option that contradicts it. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P01-003 · Knowledge and belief · passage Easy · 430 words · 4 questions

When two experts disagree about a question the rest of us cannot assess, it can seem that the layperson has only two options: to become an expert herself, or to pick a side at random. Neither is attractive. The first is usually impossible within the time available, and the second makes a mockery of the idea that belief should answer to evidence. But the dilemma is false. Although the layperson cannot judge the dispute directly, she can often judge the disputants, and judgements of that kind can be reasonable even when the subject matter remains opaque to her.

Several kinds of evidence are available. She can ask whether either expert has a record of predictions that can be checked, and how those predictions fared. She can ask whether one of them stands to gain from being believed, through money, reputation or loyalty to an institution. She can ask how the rest of the field divides, bearing in mind that a majority counts for more when its members reached their views independently than when they all learned them from the same teacher. None of these questions requires understanding the dispute itself. Each concerns the circumstances in which a claim is advanced rather than its technical content, and each can be answered, at least roughly, by an attentive citizen with no specialised training whatever.

One tempting source of evidence is less useful than it looks. Laypeople often favour the expert who argues more fluently, answers objections more quickly and seems more at ease. Yet these are skills of performance, and they are only loosely connected with being right. A careful scientist may hedge, pause and admit uncertainties that a confident charlatan would brush aside, and an audience that rewards confidence is, in effect, rewarding the very habit that should make it suspicious. The difficulty is compounded by the fact that public debate, with its brief exchanges and its appetite for memorable phrases, is arranged almost entirely so as to display performance, and so to favour the disputant who has mastered it.

It would be a mistake to conclude that the layperson's position is comfortable. Track records are often unavailable for the questions that matter most, interests can be hidden, and a field can be captured by a fashion that makes its consensus less independent than it appears. The indirect methods narrow the range of reasonable belief; they rarely close it. Still, a choice made on such grounds is not a coin toss, and treating it as one hands an advantage to whoever is most willing to exploit the public's sense of helplessness.

CAT-VARC-P01-003-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a majority of experts counts for more when
  1. (A)its members were all trained by the most eminent teacher working in that particular field.
  2. (B)it outnumbers the dissenting minority by a wide margin and has done so for a long time.
  3. (C)its members defend their position more fluently and confidently than the dissenters can.
  4. (D)its members arrived at their views independently of one another rather than from one common source.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 says 'a majority counts for more when its members reached their views independently than when they all learned them from the same teacher'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says the opposite: a majority counts for less when its members 'all learned them from the same teacher'.

Why not (B): The size and duration of a majority are never discussed; the passage makes its weight depend on independence.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 treats fluency and confidence as 'only loosely connected with being right', so they do not add weight.

Common misconception: Assuming that the size of a majority, rather than its independence, is what matters.

Difficulty: Located in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-003-Q2 · Except / not supported · EasyMCQ
The passage mentions each of the following as evidence a layperson can use in judging between disagreeing experts EXCEPT

  2. (B)the number of years each expert has spent working in the field concerned.
  3. (C)how the other members of the field divide on the question in dispute between them.
  4. (D)whether either expert stands to gain money or reputation from being believed by the public.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 lists three kinds of evidence: track record of predictions, interest in being believed, and how the field divides. Length of experience is never mentioned.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 lists this first: 'whether either expert has a record of predictions that can be checked, and how those predictions fared'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 lists this third: 'how the rest of the field divides'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 lists this second: whether one 'stands to gain from being believed, through money, reputation or loyalty to an institution'.

CAT-VARC-P01-003-Q3 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The contrast between the careful scientist and the confident charlatan in paragraph 3 serves to
  1. (A)argue that experts ought to hedge their claims and admit uncertainty more often than they now do.
  2. (B)illustrate how an expert's financial or institutional interests can be hidden from the public.
  3. (C)explain why an expert's fluency and ease are poor guides to whether that expert is right.
  4. (D)suggest that scientists are, on the whole, less trustworthy than other kinds of experts on most questions.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says fluency and ease are 'skills of performance' that are 'only loosely connected with being right'; the scientist who hedges and the charlatan who brushes doubts aside show how an audience that rewards confidence can be misled.

Why not (A): The passage gives no advice to experts; it warns audiences against rewarding confidence.

Why not (B): Hidden interests are mentioned in paragraph 4; the contrast in paragraph 3 concerns manner of argument, not interests.

Why not (D): The careful scientist is the more trustworthy figure in the contrast; the passage makes no comparison between scientists and other experts.

Common misconception: Reading the contrast as advice to experts rather than a warning to audiences.

CAT-VARC-P01-003-Q4 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely agree that the indirect methods described in paragraph 2
  1. (A)can make some lay beliefs about an expert dispute more reasonable than others without guaranteeing a correct choice.
  2. (B)allow a layperson to become as certain about the dispute as the experts themselves are about it.
  3. (C)are of no use when the question in dispute is among the questions that matter most to the public.
  4. (D)are most useful to a layperson who can also follow the technical arguments that each expert puts forward.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 says the methods 'narrow the range of reasonable belief; they rarely close it', and that a choice made on such grounds 'is not a coin toss'. They improve the reasonableness of lay belief without settling the dispute.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says the methods 'narrow the range of reasonable belief; they rarely close it', which rules out expert-level certainty.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says track records are 'often unavailable' for such questions, which concerns one method, not all of them, and stops well short of calling the methods useless.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says 'None of these questions requires understanding the dispute itself'; each concerns the circumstances of a claim 'rather than its technical content', so their value is not tied to following the technical case.

Common misconception: Overreading the qualifications of paragraph 4 into a claim that the methods are useless.

Difficulty: Requires holding the claim of paragraph 1 and the qualification of paragraph 4 together; one wrong option exaggerates the qualification. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-004 · Knowledge and belief · passage Difficult · 470 words · 4 questions

Suppose you and a colleague whom you regard as your equal in competence and care examine the same evidence and reach opposite conclusions. Neither of you has information the other lacks; neither has reason to think the other was tired or distracted. What should you do? An influential answer holds that you should move towards your colleague's view, giving her opinion the same weight as your own. The thought behind it is simple. From a neutral standpoint, the disagreement is evidence that one of you has erred, and you have no reason, independent of the dispute itself, to think the error is hers rather than yours. To stand firm would be to treat the mere fact that a conclusion is yours as a reason to favour it.

This principle seems to me correct over a wide range of cases, and it is more demanding than most people find comfortable. It condemns the familiar move of discounting a dissenter precisely because she dissents: 'she is usually so sensible, but on this question she has plainly lost her way'. If the only evidence that she has lost her way is that she disagrees with you, the reasoning simply assumes what is in question.

Yet the principle cannot be applied without qualification. Imagine two equally able auditors reviewing the same short ledger, one of whom concludes that the accounts balance while the other reports that the firm's assets exceed the number of grains of sand on the earth. The first auditor need not split the difference. Her confidence that her colleague has blundered does not rest on her own conclusion about the ledger; it rests on the absurdity of his, which she can recognise without relying on the reasoning they disagree about. What licenses holding firm, then, is not that a view is one's own but that one has grounds, independent of the dispute, for locating the error on the other side. The equal-weight principle survives, but it turns out to be a claim about what to do when such independent grounds are absent, not a rule that applies whatever one knows.

Critics sometimes press a further objection: since thoughtful people disagree about the equal-weight principle itself, the principle seems to require its own defenders to lose confidence in it. This is less damaging than it first appears. A principle about how to respond to disagreement may well recommend reduced confidence in itself without thereby becoming false; it would show only that the matter is genuinely hard, which no one denied. What the objection does reveal is that conciliation cannot be the whole story about rational belief. A thinker who revised every view the moment a peer dissented would soon have no settled views left to revise, and some account is still owed of what entitles anyone to a starting point.

CAT-VARC-P01-004-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, discounting a dissenter because she dissents is faulty reasoning because
  1. (A)it shows disrespect towards a colleague whose competence and care one has already acknowledged.
  2. (B)it uses the disputed conclusion itself as the only evidence that the dissenter has gone wrong.
  3. (C)it ignores information that the dissenter possesses and that the person discounting her lacks.
  4. (D)it gives the dissenter's view more weight than one's own view on the question under dispute.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says that 'If the only evidence that she has lost her way is that she disagrees with you, the reasoning simply assumes what is in question'.

Why not (A): The passage objects on grounds of reasoning, saying it 'assumes what is in question'; respect for colleagues is never raised.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 stipulates that 'Neither of you has information the other lacks', so ignored information is not the fault identified.

Why not (D): This reverses the move: discounting the dissenter gives her view less weight than one's own, not more.

Common misconception: Supplying a moral objection where the passage gives a logical one.

Difficulty: Stated in the last sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-004-Q2 · Structure / function · DifficultMCQ
The example of the two auditors in paragraph 3 functions in the argument primarily to
  1. (A)show that auditors and similar specialists are seldom true peers, because one of them is usually more careful than the other.
  2. (B)show that the equal-weight principle must be given up, since some disagreements between equals call for no change of view.
  3. (C)show that one may hold firm against a peer whenever one is confident that one's own conclusion is correct.
  4. (D)show that the equal-weight principle applies only where one lacks dispute-independent grounds for thinking the other party erred.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 concludes that what licenses holding firm 'is not that a view is one's own but that one has grounds, independent of the dispute, for locating the error on the other side', and that the principle is 'a claim about what to do when such independent grounds are absent'. The example restricts the principle's scope.

Why not (A): The example stipulates 'two equally able auditors'; the argument depends on their being peers, not on doubting it.

Why not (B): The author says 'The equal-weight principle survives', merely restricted in scope; the example qualifies the principle rather than refuting it.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the first auditor's confidence 'does not rest on her own conclusion about the ledger' but on the absurdity of the other's, so confidence in one's own view is not what licenses holding firm.

Common misconception: Choosing the first wrong option, which mistakes the auditor's ground for confidence in her own view, the very ground the principle forbids.

Difficulty: Two wrong options are close to the correct answer: one misidentifies the ground for holding firm, the other overstates the qualification into refutation. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P01-004-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best describes the author's view of the objection that the equal-weight principle undermines itself?
  1. (A)It is decisive, because a principle that recommends doubting itself cannot coherently be held by anyone at all.
  2. (B)It succeeds only if the principle's critics outnumber its defenders among the thoughtful people who have considered it with care.
  3. (C)It does not show that the principle is false, though it does show that conciliation cannot be all there is to rational belief.
  4. (D)It is beside the point, because the principle governs disagreements about particular bodies of evidence and not disagreements about principles themselves.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 4 calls the objection 'less damaging than it first appears' because reduced confidence would not make the principle false, but adds that 'What the objection does reveal is that conciliation cannot be the whole story about rational belief'. The author's view is partly dismissive and partly concessive.

Why not (A): The author says the objection is 'less damaging than it first appears' and that such a principle may recommend doubting itself 'without thereby becoming false'.

Why not (B): The passage makes no claim about counting critics and defenders; the reply turns on whether self-directed doubt makes a principle false.

Why not (D): The author never restricts the principle in this way, and grants that the objection 'does reveal' something important.

Common misconception: Missing the concession in the second half of paragraph 4 and treating the author's reply as a complete rejection.

Difficulty: The author's view has two parts, and each wrong option misstates one of them. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-004-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
In which of the following situations would the author most clearly hold that a person ought to move towards a disagreeing colleague's view?
  1. (A)Two equally careful historians, working from the same documents, date the same undated letter to different decades, and neither can fault the other's reasoning except by its conclusion.
  2. (B)Two equally careful chemists disagree about a reaction, but one of them knows that the other's sample was contaminated in a way the other has not noticed.
  3. (C)Two equally able judges disagree about a verdict, and one of them maintains that the accused was in two different cities at the same moment.
  4. (D)Two students disagree about a proof, one of whom has studied the topic for years while the other first met it in yesterday's lecture.

The principle applies to peers with the same evidence when one has 'no reason, independent of the dispute itself, to think the error is hers rather than yours' (paragraph 1), and paragraph 3 limits it to cases where 'such independent grounds are absent'. Only the historians meet both conditions: they are equals, share the evidence, and can fault each other only by the conclusion.

Why not (B): The first chemist has information the other lacks and a ground, independent of the dispute, for locating the error, which paragraph 3 says licenses holding firm.

Why not (C): The other judge can recognise the absurdity of this claim independently of the disputed reasoning, which is the auditor case of paragraph 3.

Why not (D): The students are not equals in competence, so the case falls outside the peer disagreement to which the principle applies (paragraph 1).

Common misconception: Overlooking either the requirement that the parties be peers with shared evidence or the exception for dispute-independent grounds.

Difficulty: Each wrong option violates a different condition of the principle, so all the conditions must be held in mind at once. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P01-005 · Knowledge and belief · passage Medium · 446 words · 4 questions

We tend to assume that each of us has a special and nearly infallible access to our own minds. Others must infer what I think and feel from my speech and behaviour, whereas I, it seems, am acquainted with my own mental states directly, without any need for inference or interpretation. For some states the assumption is plausible. If I sincerely report a sharp pain in my knee, it is hard to see what better evidence anyone could have, including me, about whether the pain is there. But the assumption is usually extended, without much reflection, to a quite different class of facts: the facts about why I did what I did.

Here the case for special access is far weaker. Imagine a shopper who chooses one of several identical scarves laid out in a row and who, asked why, cites the richer colour or the finer weave of the one she took. Since the scarves really are identical, her explanation cannot be true, yet it is offered sincerely and without hesitation. What she supplies is not a report of a cause she observed operating within her but a plausible reconstruction, drawn from a shared and largely unexamined theory of human motivation, of the kind anyone might give of why a person would choose a scarf. An onlooker armed with the same commonsense theory could have produced the same explanation, and would have been no more and no less likely to be right.

It is tempting to conclude from such cases that self-knowledge is an illusion and that we stand to ourselves essentially as strangers do, but that conclusion overreaches. The shopper's error concerns the causes of a past choice, and about such causes she may well be no better placed than anyone else. But there is another kind of question about which her position is not that of an observer at all. If she is asked which scarf she will wear tomorrow, or whether she thinks scarves make a sensible gift, she does not answer by studying evidence about herself; she answers by deciding, or by considering the merits of scarves. Her authority here derives from the fact that the answer is, in part, up to her.

The lesson, then, is not that the first person has no privilege but that its privilege has been misplaced. We are poorly placed to discover, by looking inward, the forces that moved us; we are well placed to settle what we will stand by. Confusing the two invites a double error: an unearned confidence about our own motives, and an exaggerated scepticism that would deny us even the authority we exercise whenever we make up our minds.

  4. (D)she gave it without hesitation, which shows that she had rehearsed it in advance.

Common misconception: Reading the detail about hesitation as evidence of insincerity.

CAT-VARC-P01-006 · Knowledge and belief · passage Easy · 415 words · 4 questions

'I did not know' is among the oldest excuses there is, and often among the best. We do not blame a pharmacist who dispenses a medicine that was contaminated at the factory in a way no inspection could detect, nor a traveller who carries a parcel for a friend without any reason to suspect what it contains. In such cases ignorance breaks the link between what a person did and what she intended, and blame that disregarded this would be blame for bad luck.

But the excuse has a counterfeit form. Consider a manager who tells his staff that he wants results and does not want to hear how they are achieved. When it later emerges that the results were achieved by falsifying safety records, he can say, truthfully, that he did not know. Yet his not knowing was not something that happened to him; it was something he arranged. He foresaw that there might be facts he would be obliged to act on, and he took steps to ensure that he would never be in possession of them. To accept his excuse would be to reward precisely the foresight that makes him responsible.

The distinction, then, is not between knowing and not knowing but between ignorance a person suffers and ignorance a person cultivates. The cultivated kind does not cancel responsibility; if anything, it adds a second fault to the first, since the person has both allowed a wrong to occur and taken care to shield himself from ever having to confront it. This is why such a person can seem, on reflection, no better and perhaps rather worse than someone who knew the facts plainly and still did nothing about them.

One should not push this too far. No one can investigate everything, and a demand that we trace the full history of every product we buy and every institution we deal with would be impossible to meet and would make ordinary life unworkable. Routine purchases, everyday dealings with banks and employers, and the countless small transactions of daily life proceed, quite properly, on the assumption that things are roughly as they appear. The charge of cultivated ignorance is fair only where a person had some particular reason to suspect a problem and chose not to look because looking might oblige him to act. Without such a reason, not inquiring is simply the normal condition of finite beings, and it is no more blameworthy than failing to read every book in a library.

  3. (C)suggest that most appeals to ignorance are counterfeit and should be met with suspicion by those who hear them.
  4. (D)show that people who handle goods on behalf of others ought to inspect those goods far more carefully.

Common misconception: Reading the examples as a call for vigilance rather than as cases of legitimate excuse.

Difficulty: The function is stated in the sentences around the examples. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-007 · Knowledge and belief · passage Medium · 587 words · 5 questions

Intellectual humility is praised more often than it is defined, and the praise tends to blur it with other things. Sometimes it is equated with low confidence, as if the humble thinker were one who held every opinion loosely. Sometimes it is identified with deference, the willingness to yield to those who know more. And sometimes it means no more than a modest manner, the habit of prefacing one's claims with 'I may be wrong, but'. Each of these captures something, yet none of them can be the virtue itself, because each can be present where humility is absent and absent where humility is present.

Take low confidence first. A person may be unsure of everything out of sheer timidity, believing too little rather than too much, and timidity of this kind is no more admirable than arrogance; it is merely a different way of misjudging one's evidence. Conversely, someone may be entirely certain that the earth orbits the sun, and rightly so, without any failure of humility. Deference fares no better. It is often wise, but a thinker who deferred to every apparent authority would be credulous rather than humble, and one who refused to defer to a genuine expert on a question outside her competence might be guilty of several faults without its being clear that humility is the one she lacks. As for the modest manner, it concerns how one presents oneself to others, and it can coexist with a private conviction that one is never mistaken. The phrase 'I may be wrong' is cheap to say.

A more promising account locates humility in a person's relation to her own limitations. The humble thinker is one who notices the gaps in her knowledge, the places where her reasoning is shaky and the biases to which she is prone, and who takes these into account: she seeks out the evidence she lacks, rechecks the steps she is unsure of, and discounts the conclusions she has special reason to want. This is compatible with great confidence where her grounds are good. Indeed, it is partly because she is alert to where her grounds are poor that her confidence elsewhere deserves to be taken seriously. Her humility, on this view, is not a feeling about herself but a settled pattern of attention and correction, visible chiefly in what she does whenever her own judgement is at stake.

It may be objected that this account makes humility indistinguishable from good reasoning in general. Anyone who reasons well, after all, attends to the strength of her evidence. But the two can come apart. A scientist may be superb at weighing evidence about the world and yet curiously blind to the ways in which her own ambitions shape which questions she asks and which results she finds convincing. Her failure is not a failure to reason about stars or cells; it is a failure to treat herself as one more possible source of error. That reflexive attention, directed at the reasoner rather than at the subject matter, is what the word humility usefully picks out.

The account has a further merit: it explains why humility can be overdone. A person preoccupied with her own limitations, forever cataloguing the ways in which she might be mistaken, may become unable to act on what she knows or to contribute what she has to offer. Owning one's limitations includes keeping them in proportion, and the thinker who is paralysed by self-scrutiny has misjudged them as surely as the one who ignores them.

CAT-VARC-P01-008 · Knowledge and belief · passage Difficult · 582 words · 5 questions

It is natural to think of understanding as knowledge in a higher concentration. On this view, a person who understands why bridges stand or why prices rise simply knows more than one who does not: more facts, and in particular more facts about causes. Understanding would then be knowledge of a special subject matter, the reasons why, but it would be knowledge all the same, answerable to the same standards and vulnerable to the same defeats.

The view has difficulty with a familiar figure, the student who has memorised the correct explanation. Asked why the seasons change, she replies, accurately, that the earth's axis is tilted relative to the plane of its orbit, so that each hemisphere receives more direct sunlight for part of the year. She has the answer from a reliable textbook and a reliable teacher, and by most accounts of knowledge she knows why the seasons change. Yet ask her what would happen if the tilt were greater, or why the seasons are reversed between the hemispheres, and she is at a loss. She possesses the explanation as a sentence rather than as something she can use. What she lacks is not a further fact but a grasp of how the facts she already has depend on one another, the ability to follow the explanation into cases for which it was not stated.

If this is right, understanding is less a matter of what one has been told than of what one can do with it, and this has a surprising consequence. Knowledge is usually held to require truth: one cannot know what is false. Understanding seems more forgiving. Much of what we rightly call scientific understanding is achieved through models that are, taken literally, false. The simple model of a gas treats its molecules as points that occupy no space and exert no forces on one another except in collision, which no actual molecule does; yet a student who has mastered that model understands a great deal about why gases behave as they do, and understands it partly because the model leaves out complications that would obscure the pattern. It would be odd to say that her understanding is defective merely because the model is false, and odder still to say that someone who had memorised a more accurate description, but could do nothing with it, understood more.

This should not be taken to mean that understanding floats free of truth altogether. An idealisation earns its place only when its departures from reality are irrelevant to the questions it is used to answer; the point-particle model serves well for gases at ordinary densities and misleads at very high ones, and part of understanding the model is knowing where it stops working. A story that got the dependencies wrong, however fluently it could be manipulated, would yield not understanding but a skilful illusion of it. The claim, then, is not that falsehood is harmless but that the relation between understanding and truth is looser and more selective than the relation between knowledge and truth: understanding demands accuracy in the respects that bear on the dependencies grasped, and tolerates distortion elsewhere.

The distinction matters beyond philosophy. Educational systems that measure success by the reproduction of correct statements will reward the memorising student and may never detect what she is missing, since what she is missing shows itself only when the question changes. Testing for understanding requires, at least in part, asking questions the student has not been prepared for.

The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)showing that scientific models are false, and that science therefore yields understanding of the world but never knowledge of it.
  2. (B)criticising educational systems for testing how well students remember statements rather than how well they can apply them.
  3. (C)defending the view that understanding is knowledge of causes against the objection that some causes cannot be known.
  4. (D)arguing that understanding differs from knowledge both in requiring a usable grasp of dependencies and in its more selective relation to truth.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 states the view that understanding is knowledge of causes; paragraph 2 argues against it with the memorising student; paragraphs 3 and 4 argue that understanding tolerates selective falsehood; paragraph 5 draws a practical consequence. The correct answer captures both lines of difference.

Why not (A): The passage says many models are 'taken literally, false', but never claims science yields no knowledge; this extends a point about models into a sweeping conclusion.

Why not (B): This is the subject of the short final paragraph only, which draws a consequence of the main argument; it is too narrow to be the passage's concern.

Why not (C): The passage argues against the view of paragraph 1 that understanding is 'knowledge all the same', and no objection about unknowable causes is discussed.

CAT-VARC-P01-009 · Mind and self · passage Easy · 463 words · 4 questions

When a person who keeps all her appointments in a phone loses the phone, she often says that she has lost her memory, and the phrase is less of an exaggeration than it sounds. For a growing number of thinkers, the boundary of the mind is not the skin or the skull but the reach of the processes that do a mind's work. If a notebook is consulted as automatically as recollection, trusted as fully and available as readily, then, the argument goes, the information it holds is as much a part of its owner's beliefs as anything stored in the brain. To insist otherwise is, on this view, a mere prejudice in favour of the inside.

The argument has real force, and much of it should be accepted. It is hard to say what principled difference there is between a fact that I must work to recall and a fact that I can retrieve, with less effort, from a list I always carry. Both are stored, both are retrieved, and both guide what I do. Ordinary practice already reflects this: we hold someone responsible for knowing what is written in the diary she relies on, and we speak of a shopping list as remembering things for us. When a skill depends on a tool, as a carpenter's feel for the wood depends on a plane he has used for years, the tool can seem less an object he handles than a part of how he perceives.

Yet the stronger conclusion, that the mind simply is whatever system includes the tool, moves too quickly. Devices differ from memory in a way the argument tends to pass over: they can be edited by other people. A notebook can be altered while its owner sleeps; the contents of a phone can be changed by whoever manages the service on which it depends. Biological memory is fallible, and it too can be distorted by suggestion, but it is not open in the same routine way to the revisions of strangers. The difference is not one of storage but of authority, of who has the standing to change what a person takes to be true.

The better lesson, then, is not that minds are everywhere but that trust has become a part of cognition. Once we lean on external stores, the question of whether our beliefs are sound becomes partly a question about the institutions and people who maintain those stores. That is why the loss, or the quiet alteration, of a device can feel like an injury to the self and not merely an inconvenience. The thesis that the mind extends into its tools is right to notice the injury; it is wrong to think that calling the device a part of the mind explains it.

  2. (B)show that biological memory is more trustworthy than notebooks and phones because it cannot be distorted by anyone.
  3. (C)defend the claim that the mind includes every device its owner consults, against critics who would confine it to the brain.
  4. (D)warn people who keep their appointments in phones that strangers are likely to tamper with what they have stored.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 accepts much of the argument ('much of it should be accepted'), paragraph 3 rejects the stronger conclusion because devices can be edited by others, and paragraph 4 states 'The better lesson': 'trust has become a part of cognition'. The purpose is to accept part of the thesis and redirect its lesson.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says biological memory 'is fallible, and it too can be distorted by suggestion'; the contrast drawn is about who can revise it, not about immunity to distortion.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says this 'stronger conclusion' 'moves too quickly', and paragraph 4 says calling the device part of the mind explains nothing, so the author opposes it.

Why not (D): The passage gives no warning or advice and never says tampering is likely; the phone example in paragraph 1 opens a philosophical question about the boundary of the mind.

Common misconception: Taking the thesis the author reports in paragraph 1 for the author's own conclusion.

Difficulty: The author's stance is announced in signposted sentences ('much of it should be accepted', 'Yet', 'The better lesson'). · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-010 · Mind and self · passage Easy · 431 words · 4 questions

A man resolves to give up sweets, believes sincerely that he should, and then, at the end of a long and exhausting day, eats the cake anyway. The episode is so ordinary that its strangeness is easy to miss. If he really judged that refusing was better, what moved him to do otherwise? One very old answer is that nothing did: no one knowingly chooses the worse course, and the man must have ceased to believe, at least temporarily, that refusing was better. Most people find this answer too clever by half. They are convinced that they have acted against their own considered judgement, and they describe the experience in the vocabulary of force, of being overcome by something stronger than resolution.

The popular picture that develops from this description treats the will as a kind of muscle. Judgement indicates the direction, and the will either possesses the strength to follow or it does not; lapses are failures of strength, to be corrected by training, rest or improved nutrition. The picture has the merit of matching how lapses feel, and it explains why they cluster at the end of tiring days. But it makes the lapse strangely external to the person, as though desire were a weight imposed from outside and the self merely a lifter who happened to be exhausted that particular evening.

Closer examination of such moments suggests a different interpretation. The man with the cake does not usually go on thinking, clearly and vividly, that he should refuse while his hand reaches out. Instead his attention narrows: the considerations favouring refusal, which seemed compelling in the morning, become abstract and distant, while the cake is immediate, particular and present. He does not abandon his judgement so much as stop looking at it. On this interpretation the lapse is neither a mere deficiency of strength nor a simple change of mind but a failure to keep one's own reasons in view.

This does not mean that fatigue is irrelevant, or that every lapse is a matter of attention; some temptations are simply strong, and some people are considerably more depleted than others. But the attentional account has a practical advantage. If lapses were exclusively failures of strength, the only remedy would be additional strength. If they are often failures of attention, the remedy can be arrangement: keeping one's reasons concrete, placing the cake out of sight, deciding in advance what one will do when the moment arrives. People who manage their weaknesses successfully tend to do precisely this, and the muscle picture has trouble explaining why such strategies work.

  4. (D)show that most people lack the willpower needed to keep their resolutions about food.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 says the episode's 'strangeness is easy to miss' and asks 'what moved him to do otherwise?'; paragraphs 2 to 4 then weigh answers to that question. The example sets up the puzzle.

Why not (A): No paragraph says tiredness corrupts judgement; paragraph 3 says the man 'does not abandon his judgement so much as stop looking at it'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 reports that view as 'One very old answer' that most people find 'too clever by half', and paragraph 3 rejects the idea that the lapse is 'a simple change of mind'.

Why not (D): The passage makes no claim about how many people lack willpower; the example is used to raise a puzzle about acting against one's judgement.

Common misconception: Treating the opening example as evidence for one of the views instead of as the puzzle all the views address.

CAT-VARC-P01-011 · Mind and self · passage Medium · 423 words · 4 questions

The traditional problem of other minds begins from an asymmetry. I am aware of my own pain directly, the argument runs, but I know of yours only through what you do: the wince, the cry, the hand withdrawn abruptly from the stove. How, then, am I entitled to believe that anything lies behind your behaviour at all? The classic reply is an argument from analogy. In my own case I observe that pain is regularly accompanied by wincing and crying; when I observe wincing and crying in you, I infer that pain is present in you as well, concealed from me but similar to my own.

Critics have long pointed out how fragile this inference is. It generalises from a single case to every other person, which is the kind of reasoning we would reject anywhere else: nobody who had examined one seed from a sack and found it fertile would conclude with any confidence that every seed in the sack would germinate. Worse, the conclusion can never be independently checked. In ordinary induction I can at least test a generalisation against further cases, but I can never step into your experience to confirm that the pain I inferred is really there.

These objections are sound, but they share the assumption they attack: that knowledge of other minds must be an inference from bodily movements to concealed inner states. That assumption does not describe how such knowledge actually arises. A child does not first register a contraction of facial muscles and then hypothesise distress; she sees that her mother is upset, and she sees it as immediately as she sees that the cup is full. Expressions are not evidence from which feelings are deduced. They are, in a great many cases, the very form in which feelings are visible to others, and to treat them as mere outward signs is to describe the face as a mask before asking what lies behind it.

None of this shows that we never reason about other minds. When a stranger's expressions are unfamiliar, when someone is deliberately concealing what she feels, or when we ask whether an octopus or a newborn experiences pain, we do weigh evidence and draw conclusions, and we can be badly wrong. But these are special cases, and they are intelligible only against a background in which most minds are simply seen. The sceptic's mistake is to take the hard case as the model for every case, and then to wonder why the model will not support the confidence we ordinarily have.

CAT-VARC-P01-011-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)a link between pain and behaviour that one has observed in one's own case.
  3. (C)what the other person reports, in words, about the feelings she is having.
  4. (D)a large number of observations of many different people who were in pain.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1: 'In my own case I observe that pain is regularly accompanied by wincing and crying; when I observe wincing and crying in you, I infer that pain is present in you as well'.

Why not (A): That is the author's own alternative in paragraph 3, offered against the inferential picture, not the argument from analogy.

Why not (C): Verbal reports are never mentioned; paragraph 1 says the inference rests on what one observes 'In my own case' together with the other's wincing and crying.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the argument 'generalises from a single case', which is exactly why critics call it fragile.

Common misconception: Confusing the argument from analogy with the author's perceptual alternative.

Difficulty: The argument is stated explicitly in paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-011-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author's comparison between seeing that the mother is upset and seeing 'that the cup is full' suggests that
  1. (A)recognising another person's feeling can be as direct as perceiving an ordinary fact about an object.
  2. (B)children learn to read emotions by first reasoning carefully about the behaviour of physical objects.
  3. (C)children understand other people's emotions before they understand facts about physical objects.
  4. (D)feelings, like the contents of a cup, can be measured precisely by a sufficiently careful observer.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3: 'she sees that her mother is upset, and she sees it as immediately as she sees that the cup is full.' The comparison makes the point that expressions are not 'evidence from which feelings are deduced' but the form in which feelings are visible.

Why not (B): This reverses the point: paragraph 3 denies that a child does 'first register a contraction of facial muscles and then hypothesise distress'; she sees the feeling 'as immediately' as the cup's state.

Why not (C): The comparison concerns how direct the two kinds of seeing are, not which is learned first; no order of development is discussed.

Why not (D): Nothing is said about precise measurement; paragraph 4 even grants that we 'can be badly wrong' in hard cases.

Common misconception: Reading the comparison as a claim about developmental order or precision rather than about immediacy.

Difficulty: Requires connecting the image to the claim it supports in the next two sentences. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-011-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most seriously undermine the author's reply to the sceptic?
  1. (A)People frequently misjudge what strangers from unfamiliar backgrounds are feeling, even when they try hard to understand them.
  2. (B)Some people can keep a calm and composed face while concealing intense grief or anger from those around them.
  3. (C)Young children recognise their own mothers' feelings much more quickly than they recognise the feelings of strangers.
  4. (D)Adults turn out to identify what others feel only by consciously working from observed movements to conclusions about hidden states.

Answer: (D)

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 lists deliberate concealment among the special cases in which we must weigh evidence; it does not touch the claim that most feelings are simply seen.

Why not (C): This is consistent with the author's view, since familiar expressions are the clearest cases of seeing; paragraph 4 treats unfamiliar faces as the harder cases.

Common misconception: Choosing an option about errors in reading minds, which feels like a blow to the author but describes cases paragraph 4 already concedes.

Difficulty: Two wrong options describe real limits on mind reading and the third is a plausible fact about children; the solver must see that each is compatible with paragraph 4's concession and only the correct answer attacks the general claim. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P01-012 · Mind and self · passage Medium · 442 words · 4 questions

For a long time emotions were cast as the adversaries of reason, surges of feeling that cloud judgement and must be governed by it. A more recent and considerably more flattering view inverts this picture. Emotions, it holds, are themselves judgements, or are at least constructed around them. To be afraid of a dog is to judge that the dog is dangerous; to feel indignant is to regard oneself as having been treated unjustly. The view has an obvious appeal. It explains why emotions can be appropriate or inappropriate to their circumstances, why we can sometimes argue a friend out of her jealousy by showing her that she has misread a situation, and why emotions are directed at objects rather than being mere sensations, like an itch or a cramp, that are about nothing at all.

The difficulty arises from emotions that persist against judgement. A traveller may know perfectly well that flying is far safer than the drive to the airport, may say so and sincerely believe it, and still grip the armrest in terror as the aircraft climbs. If fear were a judgement that flying is dangerous, the traveller would be simultaneously affirming and denying the same proposition, and her condition would be a straightforward contradiction. Yet it does not feel like a contradiction, nor do we treat it as one; we recognise it as a familiar kind of inner conflict, unpleasant but not absurd.

A better model is perception. When two lines of equal length are drawn with arrowheads pointing in opposite directions, one continues to look longer even after a ruler has shown that they are equal. Seeing is not believing, so the conflict involves no contradiction. Emotions, on this model, are something like perceptions of value: fear presents the climbing aircraft as dangerous in the way the illusion presents one line as longer. Such presentations are usually reliable and usually agree with our considered views, which is why the judgement theory seemed so plausible. When they disagree, we have the emotional counterpart of an optical illusion.

The analogy should not be pressed too far. We do not blame anyone for seeing the lines as unequal, but we do sometimes criticise people for their fears and resentments, and we expect them to work on them. This suggests that emotions, unlike most perceptions, are partly answerable to the person who has them: they can be reshaped over time through reflection and repeated experience, even if they cannot be dismissed by a single act of judgement. The perceptual model explains why emotional conflict is not contradiction; it must be supplemented to explain why it is nevertheless an occasion for responsibility.

CAT-VARC-P01-013 · Mind and self · passage Medium · 476 words · 4 questions

A long tradition in philosophy, going back at least to Locke, holds that what makes me the same person as some earlier individual is neither the persistence of my body nor that of some hidden soul but memory. I am the person who sat that examination twenty years ago because I can remember sitting it, from the inside, as something I did. The view has an attractive modesty. It ties personal identity to something we actually experience rather than to an unobservable substance, and it appears to match our intuitions about imaginary cases: if all of my memories were somehow transferred into another body, many people feel that it is I who would wake up in it.

The theory faces two well-known difficulties. The first concerns chains of memory. Imagine a woman of eighty who vividly remembers her first day as a teacher at thirty, and suppose that the teacher of thirty could still recall a childhood accident at six of which the woman of eighty remembers nothing. On a strict memory criterion the old woman is the teacher and the teacher is the child, yet the old woman is not the child, since she remembers nothing of her. Identity cannot behave this way: if A is the same as B and B is the same as C, then A is the same as C. The second difficulty is subtler. To distinguish a genuine memory of sitting an examination from a vivid but false impression of having sat it, we seem to need to know already that the rememberer is the person who sat it. Memory, apparently, presupposes the identity it was supposed to explain.

The first objection can be met by allowing overlapping chains of memory, rather than direct memory, to carry identity; the second is much harder to escape. Yet it is worth asking what the defenders of the memory view were really after. Their deepest conviction was not a thesis about the logic of identity but a claim about what makes the past matter to a person. A past that I can recall, whose intentions I have carried out and whose anxieties still shape me, is mine in a way that a past I share only bodily is not.

Seen in this light, the theory fails as an account of identity and succeeds as an account of something else: the degree to which a person is connected to her own history. That connection comes in degrees and can weaken, which identity, being all or nothing, cannot do. The old woman is the child she once was, but she is far less connected to that child than to the teacher. Many of our practical concerns, from the keeping of old promises to the sense of responsibility for what we did long ago, may track this connection more closely than they track identity itself.

  2. (B)memories might be transferred into another body, which would make two quite different people one and the same person.
  3. (C)identity must hold from one end of a chain to the other, whereas direct memory need not.
  4. (D)telling a genuine memory from a convincing false one seems to require knowing already who the rememberer is.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2: to tell a genuine memory from 'a vivid but false impression of having sat it, we seem to need to know already that the rememberer is the person who sat it. Memory, apparently, presupposes the identity it was supposed to explain.'

Why not (A): Forgetting childhood figures only in the first difficulty's example; the second difficulty concerns distinguishing genuine from false memories.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 cites memory transfer as an imaginary case the theory appears to handle well, not as a difficulty for it.

Why not (C): This is the first difficulty, about chains and the rule that if A is B and B is C then A is C, not the second.

Common misconception: Mixing up the two difficulties, which are presented one after the other in the same paragraph.

Difficulty: Located in the last three sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-014 · Mind and self · passage Medium · 411 words · 4 questions

Philosophical discussions of ethics characteristically concentrate on the moment of decision. A situation is described, the available options are set out, and the question is which of them a good person would choose. This framing is natural for the purposes of argument, but it quietly assumes that the morally significant work begins only once the situation has been described. In ordinary life, nobody hands us the description. What we take the situation to be, which features stand out and which recede into the background, is settled largely by what we attend to, and that settling is accomplished before any choice is considered.

Consider a manager who prides herself on her fairness. When a request for leave arrives, she weighs it conscientiously against the requirements of the team, and she applies identical rules to everyone. Yet for months she has failed to notice that one reserved employee has grown withdrawn and anxious, while she registered immediately when a more assertive colleague appeared dissatisfied. Her decisions, examined individually, may be beyond reproach. The defect lies upstream, in a pattern of attention that has made some people's difficulties vivid to her and rendered others' invisible.

It may be objected that this demands too much of ordinary moral agents. Noticing is not something we do at will; we cannot be blamed for failing to perceive what never came to our notice, any more than for failing to hear a sound pitched too high for human ears. There is something genuinely correct in the objection. No single lapse of attention is a choice, and it would be unjust to treat it as though it were. But attention is not fixed in the way the range of hearing is. It is formed by what we have habitually looked for, by whom we have taken seriously, and by the stories we tell ourselves about the people around us, and these are things over which, across time, we have considerable influence.

The upshot is that moral improvement is frequently less a matter of resolving to choose better than of learning to see better. A person who wishes to be fair must ask not only whether her decisions follow sound principles but also whose circumstances reach her as calls for decision at all. This is slower and less dramatic work than heroic choice, and it rarely earns admiration, since what it changes is largely invisible from outside. It is nonetheless where a great deal of moral life actually takes place.
