CAT-VARC-P01-015 · Mind and self · passage Difficult · 617 words · 5 questions

Arguments about free will customarily proceed in a fixed order. First we are asked to settle a question about the world: whether every event, including every human choice, is determined by antecedent causes. Only then, it is assumed, can we know whether anyone is genuinely responsible for anything, and therefore whether blame and praise are ever deserved. If the causal question is answered one way, our practices of holding one another responsible are vindicated; if it is answered the other way, those practices are exposed as resting on an illusion, and intellectual honesty requires us to abandon them.

An influential alternative reverses this order. It begins not with metaphysics but with the attitudes we actually adopt toward one another: resentment when we are injured through someone's indifference, gratitude when we are helped through their goodwill, indignation on behalf of others who have been mistreated. These attitudes, the alternative maintains, are not conclusions derived from a theory about causation. They are constitutive of what it is to live among persons rather than merely alongside objects, and to suppose that a philosophical thesis could oblige us to relinquish them is to misunderstand both the thesis and ourselves. We do suspend them in particular cases: when someone acted under duress, or did not know what he was doing, or is a young child. But these suspensions are made within the practice, for reasons the practice itself recognises, and none of them amounts to a general verdict on human agency.

There is much to admire in this reversal. It explains why the free will debate so often seems unreal to people outside it, who continue resenting and forgiving whatever the latest argument concludes, and it rightly insists that responsibility is something we do together rather than a concealed property awaiting detection. Responsibility, on this picture, resembles a language more than a fact of nature: it is sustained by shared expectations, and its rules are learned by taking part in it. The reversal also shows why the most sweeping sceptical arguments overreach: from the bare claim that choices have causes, nothing follows about whether a particular person was acting under duress, or in ignorance, or as a child acts.

Yet the reversal is sometimes taken to establish more than it can. If the practice is answerable only to itself, it may seem immune to criticism, and its present configuration may seem beyond question. The history of the practice tells against such complacency. Over time, many societies have come to regard certain compulsions, illnesses and conditions of upbringing as diminishing responsibility where they were formerly treated as plain wickedness. These revisions were not imposed by metaphysics; they arose when people learned more about how particular capacities can fail, and then applied excusing principles the practice already contained to cases it had previously judged harshly.

This is where the sciences of the mind may matter after all, though not in the way the traditional debate supposed. No general thesis about determinism will tell us whether blame is ever deserved. But detailed findings about how self-control develops, or how it is impaired, can reveal that the conditions our practice already counts as excusing are more common, or less conspicuous, than we had assumed. A discovery that some apparent callousness is in fact an inability to register the distress of others, for instance, would not refute the practice; it would require the practice to be consistent with itself. The practice of holding responsible is neither a fixed point beyond inquiry nor a conclusion awaiting metaphysical proof. It is a body of standards that can be held to its own commitments, and inquiry into the mind is one of the ways in which that is done.

CAT-VARC-P01-015-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the alternative view described in paragraph 2, attitudes such as resentment and gratitude are
  1. (A)feelings that a careful moral philosopher should try to exclude from judgements of blame.
  2. (B)part of living among persons, not conclusions drawn from a theory of causation.
  3. (C)suspended whenever it can be shown that a person's choice had antecedent causes.
  4. (D)justified only after the question of whether every choice is determined has been settled.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: 'These attitudes, the alternative maintains, are not conclusions derived from a theory about causation. They are constitutive of what it is to live among persons rather than merely alongside objects'.

Why not (A): The alternative treats these attitudes as 'constitutive' of life among persons and warns against supposing a thesis could oblige us to 'relinquish them'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says suspensions occur in particular cases such as duress or ignorance, and 'none of them amounts to a general verdict on human agency'; causes as such are never a ground.

Why not (D): That is the traditional order of paragraph 1, which the alternative 'reverses'.

Common misconception: Confusing the alternative view with the traditional order set out in paragraph 1.

Difficulty: Stated directly in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-016 · Mind and self · passage Difficult · 579 words · 5 questions

It has become a commonplace, in philosophy as much as in popular psychology, that a self is a kind of story. On this view a person is not a thing that persists beneath her experiences but the narrative she composes from them: the account of where she came from, what she has struggled against and where she is heading. The claim is usually advanced in two forms at once. The descriptive form says that human beings do in fact understand themselves through stories. The normative form adds that they ought to, and that a life which cannot be gathered into a coherent narrative is in some way impoverished or broken.

The descriptive claim is plausible for many people, but its generality is doubtful. Some people report that they do not experience their lives as a plot at all. They feel little connection with the person they were a decade ago, find the invitation to tell their life story faintly baffling, and live with their attention fixed on present tasks and relationships. Nor is there any obvious sense in which their lives go worse: they form attachments, deliberate carefully and take responsibility for their conduct, only without experiencing their existence as an unfolding plot. It would be rash to assume that such people are simply unreflective, or that they are concealing a narrative they would rather not acknowledge. Their testimony is at least as credible as that of people who say the opposite, and it suggests that narrative self-understanding is one way of being a self rather than the condition of being one.

The normative claim is more troubling. Stories have shapes, and the pressure to give one's life a shape is a pressure to select. Episodes that fit the arc are remembered and retold; those that do not are discarded or reinterpreted. Autobiographical memory is already reconstructive, and a governing narrative supplies it with a principle of selection that is rarely examined. A person who has told herself for years that she is someone who overcomes adversity will find it difficult to see the occasions on which she gave up, and harder still to learn from them. The demand for coherence can thus become a demand for a flattering kind of forgetting. A novelist may legitimately omit whatever does not serve the plot; a life conducted on the same principle may lose precisely the evidence it most needs.

None of this means that narrative has no place. Making a promise requires regarding one's future self as continuous with one's present intention, and recovering from bereavement or injury often involves finding a way to tell what happened so that it can be lived with. Understanding other people, too, frequently depends on grasping the story they take themselves to be in. These are genuine goods, and a person who could never assemble any part of her past into an account would be at a real disadvantage.

But the goods enumerated here are those of an instrument, not of an essence. We use stories to bind ourselves, to heal and to understand, and we can use them well or badly, often or seldom. To say that the self simply is its story makes the instrument indispensable, and it turns a difference in temperament into a deficiency. The more modest view leaves room both for lives that are lived largely in the present and for the episodes that refuse to fit, and it may well be the more truthful of the two.

  2. (B)stories should be used only for making promises, recovering from loss and understanding others.
  3. (C)a person is a thing that persists unchanged beneath the experiences she has over a lifetime.
  4. (D)human beings do in fact come to understand themselves through stories about their lives.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1: 'The normative form adds that they ought to, and that a life which cannot be gathered into a coherent narrative is in some way impoverished or broken.'

Why not (B): These uses are the author's own list of genuine goods in paragraph 4, and the author never says stories should be limited to them.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says the narrative view denies exactly this: a person 'is not a thing that persists beneath her experiences'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 identifies this as 'The descriptive form'; the normative form 'adds' a claim about what people ought to do.

Common misconception: Confusing the two forms defined in consecutive sentences of paragraph 1.

Difficulty: Defined directly in paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-017 · Moral philosophy · passage Easy · 437 words · 4 questions

Two drivers leave the same party, each having had one drink too many, and each drives home along the same dark road with the same degree of carelessness. The first arrives home without incident; the second, rounding a bend, strikes a child who has run into the road. Most people judge the second driver far more harshly than the first: he may face prison, while the first, if caught at all, will pay a fine, and the second driver himself will probably carry a burden of guilt that the first never feels. Yet the only difference between them is something neither of them controlled, namely whether a child happened to be on the road at that moment.

Philosophers call this resultant moral luck, and it presents a genuine puzzle. A widely held principle says that we should be blamed only for what lies within our control. If that principle is right, the two drivers deserve exactly the same blame, since their choices, their states of mind and their recklessness were identical. Our differing reactions must then be a mistake, an emotional response to a dreadful outcome that we wrongly dress up as a moral judgement. On this view, the honest course is to blame both drivers equally, and severely, for the risk each of them imposed on others.

This conclusion is tidy, but I think it is too quick, because it treats our responses to outcomes as noise to be filtered out when they may be tracking something real. The second driver has done something the first has not: he has killed a child. His relationship to the child's family, to the world and to his own past is altered in a way that the first driver's is not. To feel and to judge that he must answer for this is not simply to confuse bad luck with bad character. It reflects the fact that agency extends into the world, and that what we are responsible for includes, in part, what our actions actually bring about.

None of this licenses the opposite error of treating the lucky driver as blameless. He did something seriously wrong, and the fact that his drive ended well is no credit to him. The point is narrower: the control principle captures one important strand of our thinking about blame, the strand concerned with assessing character and choice, but it is not the whole of moral life. A theory that cannot make sense of why the second driver should apologise to the family, and why the first has no one to apologise to, has not purified our moral concepts; it has impoverished them.

CAT-VARC-P01-017-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)argue that blame rightly responds in part to outcomes, while granting that the control principle captures one strand of moral judgement.
  2. (B)show that the second driver deserves greater blame because he drove more recklessly than the first driver did on the same road.
  3. (C)defend the principle that people should be blamed only for what lies within their control against our emotional reactions to outcomes.
  4. (D)argue that the driver who arrived home safely should be treated as blameless because his careless drive harmed no one.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 sets out the control principle and its conclusion that the drivers deserve equal blame; paragraph 3 calls this 'too quick' because responsibility includes 'what our actions actually bring about'; paragraph 4 concedes that the principle captures 'one important strand' of blame but 'is not the whole of moral life'. The correct answer states both the thesis and the concession.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the two drivers' 'recklessness were identical'; the author's case for the difference rests on the outcome, not on any difference in carelessness.

Why not (C): This is the view the author calls 'too quick' in paragraph 3; the passage argues against treating our responses to outcomes as mere emotional noise.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 rejects exactly this 'opposite error': the lucky driver 'did something seriously wrong', and his good outcome is 'no credit to him'.

Common misconception: Mistaking the view the author reports in paragraph 2 for the author's own position.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated outright in paragraphs 3 and 4; each wrong option contradicts an explicit sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-018 · Moral philosophy · passage Medium · 440 words · 4 questions

Most moral theories divide actions into three kinds: those we are required to do, those we are forbidden to do, and those that are merely permitted, yet ordinary moral thought seems to need a fourth. When a passer-by plunges into a freezing river to rescue a stranger, we do not say that she merely did something permitted, as though her act ranked alongside choosing tea over coffee. Nor do we say that she did her duty, for we would not have condemned her had she stayed on the riverbank and called for help. We say instead that she went beyond what duty asked. Acts of this kind are called supererogatory, and their existence has proved surprisingly awkward for theories that try to derive the whole of morality from a single principle.

The difficulty is sharpest for a simple form of consequentialism, the view that we are always required to do whatever produces the best outcome. On that view there is no room above duty, because the best available act is always the required one. If plunging into the river was the best thing she could do, she was obliged to do it; if it was not, she acted wrongly. Heroism disappears into ordinary obligation, and the person who fails to be a hero becomes a wrongdoer. Many critics take this to be a decisive objection: a theory that makes sainthood compulsory has misunderstood what morality is for.

Defenders reply that the objection confuses two questions: what one ought to do and what one may be blamed for not doing. Perhaps we should not blame the bystander who stays on the riverbank, since blaming people for failing to be heroes would be useless or cruel, while still holding that the rescue was what she ought to have done. This move is ingenious, and it does capture something; our reluctance to blame is partly practical. But it leaves the rescuer's own understanding of her act unexplained, for people who perform such acts often say, sincerely, that they had no choice, yet they rarely say that those who held back did wrong. Their sense of being called is personal, not a verdict on everyone.

What this suggests is that the space beyond duty is real but has a peculiar shape. It is not a zone of optional extras to which morality is indifferent, since such acts are admired precisely as moral achievements. Nor is it simply duty under a gentler name. It is the region where morality stops issuing demands to everyone and starts issuing invitations to particular people, and a theory that can speak only in demands will always misdescribe it.

CAT-VARC-P01-018-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
It can be inferred that the simple form of consequentialism described in paragraph 2 would regard a bystander who declined a rescue that was the best act available to him as
  1. (A)having acted wrongly, since on that view the best available act is always the one required.
  2. (B)having performed a supererogatory act of his own by choosing to stay on the riverbank and call for help.
  3. (C)having done nothing open to criticism, since on that view heroic rescues are never required of anyone.
  4. (D)having acted permissibly, as long as he did nothing to obstruct anyone else who attempted the rescue.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2: 'the best available act is always the required one. If plunging into the river was the best thing she could do, she was obliged to do it; if it was not, she acted wrongly.' Declining a rescue that was the best act is therefore wrong on this view.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the view leaves 'no room above duty', so it can classify no act as supererogatory, least of all the refusal to rescue.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the reverse: on this view 'the person who fails to be a hero becomes a wrongdoer'.

Why not (D): On this view the best available act is required, so any alternative to it is wrong; the passage allows no merely permissible option beside the best act.

Common misconception: Importing the ordinary view of paragraph 1 into the consequentialist view of paragraph 2.

Difficulty: The inference follows directly from two sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-018-Q2 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ

Difficulty: The remark mentions blame, which makes the defenders' point about blame a tempting but wrong home for it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-018-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following situations best fits the author's description in paragraph 4 of the region beyond duty?

  2. (B)A doctor feels that she must volunteer in an epidemic zone, though she does not think that colleagues who stay at home are at fault.
  3. (C)A lawyer handles a case without charging a fee because the court has assigned the case to him under its standing rules.
  4. (D)A shopper chooses one brand of soap over another for no reason except habit, and would be equally content with either.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 describes the region as one where morality issues 'invitations to particular people' rather than demands on everyone, and where acts are admired as moral achievements. The doctor feels personally called but issues no verdict on her colleagues, exactly the pattern of paragraph 3.

Why not (A): Paying taxes honestly is a demand that applies to everyone; the region beyond duty is where morality 'stops issuing demands to everyone'.

Why not (C): This is an act required of him by an assigned obligation, which is 'duty' in the passage's terms, not an invitation addressed to him personally.

Why not (D): This is the 'merely permitted' choice of paragraph 1, like 'choosing tea over coffee'; paragraph 4 says the region beyond duty is not one to which morality is indifferent.

Common misconception: Choosing an admirable act that is in fact a duty, such as honest tax payment.

CAT-VARC-P01-019 · Moral philosophy · passage Medium · 429 words · 4 questions

A promise is an odd moral instrument. By uttering a few words, I create an obligation that did not exist a moment before, and I hand you a kind of authority over my future conduct. Because the obligation is self-made, it can seem absolute: having bound myself freely, I have no one to blame but myself if keeping my word turns out to be inconvenient. Much of the value of promising depends on this firmness. A promise that dissolved whenever the promiser found something better to do would offer the person relying on it nothing that a statement of intention does not already provide.

Yet no one seriously believes that every promise binds whatever happens. If I promise to meet you for lunch and, on the way, come upon an injured cyclist who needs to be taken to hospital, I should miss the lunch, and you would think less of me if I did not. The usual explanation is that the promise was outweighed: the duty to help the cyclist was simply stronger. But this explanation, though natural, misses something. It implies that I had two competing obligations and chose the heavier, leaving me in your debt for the one I failed to honour. In fact, most people would say that I owe you an explanation, perhaps an apology for your wasted time, but not compensation, and certainly not a confession of wrongdoing.

A better account starts from what was actually exchanged. When you accepted my promise, you did not acquire a right to my presence at lunch under every conceivable circumstance; you acquired a right against my changing my mind for reasons of my own convenience. Promises carry an implicit understanding of the conditions under which they were made, and both parties know, without saying so, that some emergencies fall outside it. On this view the cyclist does not outweigh the promise; he reveals that the promise never extended so far.

This account has a cost, and it should be admitted. If the scope of a promise is set by unspoken understandings, then a promiser who wants to escape can always claim that his circumstances fell outside what was understood. The firmness that made promising valuable seems to leak away. The answer is that the implicit conditions are not whatever the promiser privately had in mind; they are what a reasonable person in the promisee's position would have taken them to be. That standard will not settle every case, but it keeps the exceptions public, and a promise whose limits are public remains a promise worth having.

CAT-VARC-P01-019-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
Which of the following best expresses the main point of the passage?
  1. (A)When a promise conflicts with a stronger duty, the stronger duty prevails and the promiser owes compensation for the breach.
  2. (B)The firmness of promises is an illusion, since a promiser can always claim that his situation was not foreseen.
  3. (C)Promises bind only within limits set by a shared, reasonable understanding of the circumstances in which they were made.
  4. (D)A promise must always be kept, because the person who made it freely chose to create the obligation.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 gives the author's account: a promise carries 'an implicit understanding of the conditions under which they were made', and paragraph 4 fixes those conditions by 'what a reasonable person in the promisee's position would have taken them to be'. The correct answer captures this account.

Why not (A): This is the 'outweighed' explanation that paragraph 2 says 'misses something'; the author also says compensation is not owed.

Why not (B): This is the objection raised in paragraph 4, which the author answers with the standard of the reasonable promisee.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says the obligation 'can seem absolute', but paragraph 2 at once says 'no one seriously believes that every promise binds whatever happens'.

Common misconception: Choosing the objection of paragraph 4 or the rejected explanation of paragraph 2 as the author's view.

Difficulty: Each wrong option is a position the passage states and then sets aside. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-020 · Moral philosophy · passage Difficult · 463 words · 4 questions

Moral philosophers argue with cases. A theory is proposed, someone describes a situation in which the theory delivers a verdict that strikes nearly everyone as monstrous, and the theory is revised or abandoned. The practice treats our considered reactions to cases, usually called intuitions, as something like data: not infallible, but weighty enough that a principle which contradicts too many of them loses its claim on us. Critics have long found this method suspicious. Intuitions, they point out, are the residue of upbringing, culture and evolutionary pressures that had nothing to do with tracking moral truth. To test theories against them is to test a map against the traveller's prejudices about where the roads ought to go.

The objection has force, but it proves less than it seems to. Every method of inquiry begins with some judgements it does not, at that moment, question; the empirical sciences rely on perceptual reports whose reliability can be checked only by further perception. The question is not whether intuitions have causes unrelated to truth, for every belief has causes, but whether we have specific reason to think a particular intuition distorted. Sometimes we do. An intuition that tracks a vivid feature of a case, such as whether harm is done with one's own hands, while ignoring features that on reflection seem to matter more, such as how much harm is done, earns suspicion. But suspicion of that kind is itself grounded in other intuitions, about which features matter and which do not.

This is why the familiar picture of intuitions as raw data against which theories are tested is misleading in the other direction as well. The method that actually operates is one of mutual adjustment. Principles are revised in the light of judgements about cases, and judgements about cases are revised in the light of principles that have earned our confidence elsewhere. Neither level has final authority, and the goal is a state in which the two support each other. Critics sometimes say that this is circular, and in a sense it is; but the circle is wide, and it is not obvious that any inquiry into how we should live could proceed in a straight line.

What follows is a modest but not trivial conclusion. Intuitions are neither oracles nor noise. The debunker who dismisses them all owes us an account of the standpoint from which the dismissal is made, and it is hard to find one that does not rely on some judgement of the same kind. The defender who treats them as data owes us something too: a willingness to let a strong theory overturn a widely shared reaction, as moral thought has done before, when the reaction turns out to rest on a feature of cases that should never have mattered.

CAT-VARC-P01-021 · Moral philosophy · passage Easy · 425 words · 4 questions

It is often said that forgiveness must be earned. On this view, the wrongdoer must first acknowledge what he did, express remorse and, where possible, make amends; only then is the victim in a position to forgive, and to forgive earlier would be to excuse the wrong or to collude in pretending that it did not matter. The view has an attractive moral seriousness. It refuses to let forgiveness become a soft word for forgetting, and it insists that wrongs make demands on the people who commit them.

But the view also has an awkward consequence, for it puts the victim's moral recovery in the hands of the person who harmed her. A woman wronged by a former friend who has since died, or who simply refuses to admit anything, would on this account be permanently barred from forgiving. She could stop thinking about the wrong, or decide that it no longer troubled her, but she could not forgive, because the conditions for forgiveness depend on someone else's change of heart. It is strange to make one of the most personal acts a person can perform conditional on the cooperation of the one person least likely to give it.

A better account distinguishes between what forgiveness is and what makes it fitting. To forgive is to give up resentment and the claim to repayment while continuing to judge that the act was wrong. Understood in this way, forgiveness does not excuse anything, since the judgement of wrong remains in place. What an apology does is make forgiveness easier and, often, more appropriate, because it removes the reason that resentment had for persisting: the wrongdoer who sincerely apologises has, in effect, joined the victim in condemning what he did. Without an apology, forgiveness remains possible, but it is a gift rather than a response, and it is offered for the victim's reasons rather than the wrongdoer's.

This is not to say that unconditional forgiveness is always admirable. Someone who forgives every injury at once, before she has even registered that she was wronged, may be showing not generosity but a lack of self-respect, and forgiveness that comes too easily can signal that the forgiver does not believe she was owed anything. A person who is slighted again and again by the same colleague, and who forgives each slight as though nothing had happened, may also be teaching the colleague that such slights cost him nothing, which serves neither of them. The point is only that apology is one route to forgiveness rather than its gatekeeper.

  3. (C)showing that genuine forgiveness requires the wrongdoer to acknowledge the wrong, express remorse and make amends.
  4. (D)criticising people who forgive quickly on the ground that they lack both self-respect and moral seriousness.

Paragraph 2 objects that the earned view bars the victim from forgiving an unrepentant or dead wrongdoer; paragraph 3 says that 'Without an apology, forgiveness remains possible'; paragraph 4 concludes that apology is 'one route to forgiveness rather than its gatekeeper'.

Common misconception: Mistaking the reported view of paragraph 1 for the author's thesis.

CAT-VARC-P01-022 · Moral philosophy · passage Medium · 409 words · 4 questions

Moral relativism is often defended as the natural philosophy of a tolerant person. If moral claims are true only relative to the standards of a culture, the argument runs, then no culture is in a position to condemn the practices of another, and the proper attitude towards difference is one of respectful non-interference. The appeal of this reasoning is easy to understand, since much historical cruelty was committed by people who were certain that their own customs embodied universal truth and that this certainty licensed them to remake others by force.

Yet the argument has a curious structure. Its conclusion, that we ought to tolerate other cultures, is itself a moral claim, and it is plainly meant to hold universally: the relativist does not think tolerance is required only of cultures that happen to value it. A culture whose standards prized conquest would, by relativist lights, be acting rightly when it conquered. If relativism is true, the most a tolerant culture can say to such a culture is that tolerance is valued here, which is a report about itself rather than a reason addressed to anyone else. The relativist who wants tolerance to bind everyone has quietly helped himself to a non-relative principle.

None of this shows that relativism is false, and the defender has a reply. Perhaps the claim is not that relativism entails tolerance but that believing it tends to produce tolerant people, by weakening the confidence that fuels crusades. That may be true as a matter of psychology, though the historical record is mixed; doubt can make people gentle, but it can also leave them unable to object to anything at all. More importantly, the reply changes the subject. It defends relativism as a useful belief rather than a true one, and it concedes that the case for tolerance must be made on other grounds.

Those other grounds are not hard to find. One can hold that some moral claims are true for everyone and still think that humility about one's own judgements, respect for people's capacity to govern their own lives, and the dismal record of forced conversion all count strongly against intervention in most cases. Such a view is less comfortable than relativism, because it obliges one to say that some practices are wrong even while declining to stamp them out. But it has the advantage of being able to explain why tolerance matters, which relativism, for all its tolerant intentions, cannot.

CAT-VARC-P01-022-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ

  1. (A)persuade the conquering culture to stop by showing that its own standards in fact forbid conquest.
  2. (B)do no more than report that tolerance is valued in its own culture, without giving the other a reason.
  3. (C)justify intervening by force to protect the peoples that the conquering culture threatens.
  4. (D)condemn conquest as wrong by appealing to a moral standard that both cultures are bound to accept.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: 'If relativism is true, the most a tolerant culture can say to such a culture is that tolerance is valued here, which is a report about itself rather than a reason addressed to anyone else.'

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 describes a culture 'whose standards prized conquest', so its own standards cannot be shown to forbid it.

Why not (C): Nothing in the passage says relativism licenses forcible intervention; the relativist argument is meant to support non-interference.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says that under relativism there is no such shared standard; appealing to one would mean helping oneself 'to a non-relative principle'.

Common misconception: Assuming that the tolerant culture can still appeal to a shared standard.

Difficulty: Paraphrase of one sentence in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-022-Q2 · Except / not supported · MediumMCQ
The author makes or accepts each of the following points EXCEPT that
  1. (A)the argument of paragraph 2 establishes that moral relativism is false.
  2. (B)doubt about moral matters can leave people unable to object to anything at all.

Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says doubt 'can also leave them unable to object to anything at all'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 lists 'the dismal record of forced conversion' among the grounds against intervention.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 accepts this when explaining the appeal of relativism: such certainty 'licensed them to remake others by force'.

CAT-VARC-P01-023 · Moral philosophy · passage Medium · 590 words · 5 questions

Talk of moral progress makes many people uneasy. It sounds like the self-congratulation of every age that has looked back on its predecessors and found them wanting, and it seems to require a fixed standard against which the direction of change could be measured, a standard that is itself in dispute. Yet it is hard to describe the abolition of legal slavery, or the end of public torture as a form of entertainment, merely as changes in fashion, as though they were on a par with a shift in the length of coats. Most people who doubt the idea of moral progress in theory find themselves unable to give it up in particular cases.

One influential way of making sense of the idea is to describe progress as the expansion of a circle of concern. Early moral codes, on this telling, extended full consideration only to kin or to members of the tribe; over time the boundary has been pushed outward, to strangers, to foreigners, to people of other races and faiths, and, more recently and more contentiously, to animals. Progress consists in recognising as morally considerable those whom we previously ignored. The picture is appealing because it gives progress a direction without requiring agreement on a complete moral theory: one needs only the modest premise that arbitrary exclusion is a mistake. It also explains why reformers have so often described their work as opening the eyes of their contemporaries rather than changing their values: the values, on this telling, were already present, and only their application had been blocked.

The picture is not wrong, but it is incomplete in a way that matters. Some of the clearest cases of progress involve not who counts but what we owe to those who already counted. The view that a husband may beat his wife was not, in most societies that held it, a view that wives fell outside moral concern; wives were very much inside the circle, assigned a place in it that we now regard as degrading. Reform came not from widening the boundary but from revising the terms within it. The same is true of changing attitudes to children, who have always been objects of intense concern, and whose treatment has nonetheless been transformed.

There is also a danger in the circle image that its admirers tend to overlook. If progress is measured by the width of the circle, then any extension looks like an advance, and any contraction like a regression. But concern can be extended in ways that are themselves harmful: the circle of a missionary empire was wide, and that width was part of its justification for interfering in other people's lives. Inclusion that comes with a presumption of authority over the included is not obviously better than the indifference it replaces. What matters is not only that more beings are counted, but how they are counted.

None of this means that we should abandon the idea of moral progress. It means that progress is less like a single line along which societies advance and more like a series of corrections, each prompted by the discovery that some practice depended on a mistaken belief, an unexamined interest or a failure of imagination. Such corrections can be recognised as improvements without a finished theory of the good, in the way that a scientist can recognise an error without possessing the final truth. That is a more modest claim than the circle image offers, but it is also harder to dismiss as the vanity of the present.

  2. (B)Moral progress is real but is better understood as a series of corrections than as the widening of a circle of concern.
  3. (C)Moral progress consists in extending moral concern to those beings who were previously left outside its boundary.
  4. (D)Talk of moral progress is largely the self-congratulation of each age as it looks back on the ages before it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 5 states the conclusion: progress is 'less like a single line along which societies advance and more like a series of corrections'. Paragraphs 3 and 4 show why the circle image is incomplete, and paragraph 1 establishes that progress is real in particular cases.

Why not (A): Paragraph 5 says progress is 'less like a single line along which societies advance and more like a series of corrections'.

Why not (C): This is the circle picture of paragraph 2, which paragraph 3 calls 'incomplete in a way that matters'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 reports this worry, and paragraph 5 says 'None of this means that we should abandon the idea of moral progress'.

Common misconception: Choosing the circle picture because it occupies a whole paragraph and is described as appealing.

Difficulty: The conclusion is stated in paragraph 5; the wrong options are views the passage reports or denies. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-024 · Moral philosophy · passage Difficult · 588 words · 5 questions

Virtue ethics asks not what rule an action follows or what outcome it produces but what kind of person performs it. Its central concepts are traits of character such as honesty, courage and compassion, conceived as stable dispositions that lead a person to act well across a wide range of circumstances. The compassionate person does not help only when it is convenient or when someone is watching; her compassion shows itself in the market and at home, towards friends and towards strangers. It is this breadth that distinguishes a virtue from a habit, and it is what makes the attribution of a virtue a substantial claim about a person rather than a summary of what she happened to do last week.

In recent decades a group of critics, often called situationists, has argued that this picture is empirically naive. They point to a body of experimental work in social psychology, some of it now disputed, which has been taken to show that small and morally irrelevant features of a situation, such as whether a person is in a hurry or in a pleasant mood, predict whether she will help someone in need far better than anything we know about her character. If behaviour is driven so heavily by circumstance, the critics conclude, then broad traits of the kind virtue ethics requires are rare or nonexistent, and a theory built around them describes a psychology that human beings do not have. On this view, to call someone honest or kind is less a discovery about her than a story we tell to make her conduct look more orderly than it is.

The argument is more damaging to some versions of virtue ethics than to others. If the theory claimed that most people possess the virtues, the evidence would tell heavily against it. But the classical tradition never claimed this. It held that full virtue is difficult, uncommon and achieved, if at all, through long training, and that most people are a mixture of good impulses and weak resolve. Evidence that ordinary people are easily swayed by circumstance is exactly what such a tradition would predict. Indeed, much of the advice it offers, to avoid temptations rather than trust oneself to resist them and to choose one's companions with care, makes sense only on the assumption that character is fragile.

This reply, however, has a cost that its defenders do not always acknowledge. If genuine virtue is so rare that experimental samples almost never contain it, then virtue ethics can no longer claim the advantage it has often advertised over its rivals: that it describes moral life as it is actually lived, in terms of real people and their recognisable qualities. An ideal that almost no one embodies may still be worth pursuing, but it begins to look less like a description of character and more like a rule of the kind the theory set out to replace, a standard against which conduct is measured rather than a source from which it flows.

The situationist challenge, then, does not refute virtue ethics, but it changes what the theory can coherently promise. The theory can keep its account of the ideal, or its claim to realism about ordinary people, but it is hard to see how it can keep both. Which it should give up is a question that experiments alone cannot answer, since it depends on what we want an ethical theory to do: to guide those who aspire to goodness, or to describe those whose characters are already formed.

CAT-VARC-P01-024-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says a virtue is marked by its 'breadth' across circumstances, so conduct confined to friends does not amount to the virtue.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says breadth, not sincerity, 'distinguishes a virtue from a habit'.

Common misconception: Treating sincerity or partial possession as enough for a virtue.

Difficulty: Direct application of the definition in paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P01-025 · Political philosophy · passage Easy · 493 words · 4 questions

A familiar answer to the question of why citizens owe obedience to their state is that they have agreed to it. Few of us, of course, ever signed anything, so the agreement is said to be tacit: by continuing to live within a country's borders, using its roads and accepting the protection of its courts, a person signals consent to its authority. The view is attractive because it seems to reconcile two things that are otherwise difficult to hold together, allowing us to say that political authority is genuinely binding while also maintaining that no one is bound against their will, since anyone who objects remains free to leave.

The trouble lies in that word 'free'. We do not normally count a choice as consent unless the person had a real alternative that could be taken at a bearable cost. Imagine a mining town with a single employer that also owns every shop, and suppose the company announces that anyone who keeps buying bread at its counters has thereby accepted all of its rules. No one would treat the purchase of bread as agreement, because the only way to refuse is to give up eating or to give up the town. Leaving a country is usually costlier still: it can mean abandoning one's language, one's family, one's work and the only place one knows well. There is, moreover, a further difficulty: almost every habitable stretch of the earth is now claimed by some state, so the emigrant does not escape political authority at all. She merely exchanges one authority for another, if another will admit her. The option on offer is not freedom from rule but the choice of a ruler.

Defenders of the view have a reasonable reply. Consent, they say, comes in degrees, and the availability of exit is not nothing. A state that lets its citizens leave, and lets them take their savings with them, really does treat them differently from one that seals its borders, and it seems right to say that the first has a stronger claim on their loyalty. This should be granted. A right of exit is a genuine good, and a state that denies it has failed one important test of legitimacy.

But to grant this is not to rescue the theory. That free exit is a condition of legitimacy does not make it the source of legitimacy, any more than an unlocked door makes a house a home. If a state's authority over those who stay can be justified, the justification must come from what the state does for and to them: whether its laws are fairly made, whether its burdens are fairly shared, whether its protection is extended to all. The language of tacit consent tends to obscure this question, since it allows a state to take credit for the mere fact that people have not fled, and to treat the absence of an escape as though it were an act of agreement.

  3. (C)merely living in a country cannot supply the consent that would legitimise its authority, though the freedom to leave still matters.
  4. (D)tacit consent is a sound basis for political authority provided that emigration is made cheap and easy for every citizen.

Common misconception: Seizing on the concession about exit and inflating it into the main claim.

Difficulty: The structure is signposted ('The trouble lies', 'This should be granted', 'But to grant this'), so the thesis is easy to locate. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

The sentence reads: 'The option on offer is not freedom from rule but the choice of a ruler.' It follows the point that the emigrant 'merely exchanges one authority for another', so the only choice is which authority, never none.

Why not (A): The phrase contrasts choosing a ruler with 'freedom from rule'; nothing suggests picking among laws.

Why not (C): The phrase concerns emigration, not elections; paragraph 2 is about exchanging 'one authority for another' by moving.

Why not (D): The passage says nothing about the relative strictness of states; it says only that some state's authority awaits the emigrant.

Common misconception: Reading 'choice of a ruler' as a remark about voting.

Difficulty: The phrase is explained by the sentence immediately before it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-026 · Political philosophy · passage Easy · 439 words · 4 questions

It is often said that a tolerant society is one in which people do not mind how others live, but this gets the idea almost exactly backwards. To tolerate something is to put up with it, and one can only put up with what one dislikes or disapproves of. A person who is untroubled by her neighbour's choice of diet, dress or companions is not tolerating that choice; she is simply indifferent to it, and indifference requires no effort and earns no praise. Toleration begins where objection begins: it is the decision not to act against something that one continues to regard as mistaken.

Put this way, toleration can look puzzling, even paradoxical. If I believe that a practice is wrong, how can it be a virtue for me to let it continue? Surely the better person would either stop objecting or start resisting. The puzzle dissolves, however, once we recognise that the tolerant person holds reasons at two distinct levels. At the first level she objects to the practice; at the second level she has reasons, which she judges to be weightier, for not interfering with it: that adults have authority over their own lives, that her own judgement might be wrong, that a society in which everyone enforced their disapproval on everyone else would be unbearable. Consider a mother who thinks her grown son's choice of career foolish and tells him so, but would never dream of threatening to cut him out of her will to force him back. She has not abandoned her objection. She has ranked it beneath something she values more.

Toleration so understood has limits, and they follow from the same structure. Where the second-level reasons give out, as when a practice inflicts harm on people who have not agreed to bear it, the case for restraint weakens and may disappear. A tolerant society is not one that permits everything.

Some critics find the whole idea condescending: to be tolerated, they point out, is to be told that one is disapproved of and merely endured, and they propose that a just society should aim higher, at the positive recognition and affirmation of different ways of life. There is something in this complaint, since nobody enjoys being put up with. Yet the modesty of toleration is also its strength. It asks citizens who disagree deeply to share a political life without first requiring them to approve of one another, which is fortunate, because in a diverse society such approval is not going to be forthcoming. A demand for universal affirmation sounds more generous, but it may ask for what people cannot sincerely give.

CAT-VARC-P01-026-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)Toleration is a feeling of mild dislike, whereas indifference is the absence of any feeling.
  3. (C)Toleration concerns matters of public law, whereas indifference concerns private habits like diet.
  4. (D)Toleration involves refraining from acting against something that one still regards as wrong.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 1: indifference 'requires no effort', whereas toleration 'begins where objection begins' and is 'the decision not to act against something that one continues to regard as mistaken'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 says toleration exists only where one 'continues to regard' the practice 'as mistaken', so approval is the opposite of what it requires.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 defines toleration as 'the decision not to act against' what one objects to, a choice of restraint, not a mere feeling.

Why not (C): The passage never divides the two by subject matter; diet and dress appear in paragraph 1 only as examples of what one may be indifferent to.

Common misconception: Missing that toleration is defined by a decision to hold back, not only by an attitude of dislike.

Difficulty: The definition is given directly in the last two sentences of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-027 · Political philosophy · passage Medium · 431 words · 4 questions

Theories of punishment have long been divided between those that look forward and those that look back. Forward-looking theories justify punishment by its effects: it deters, it incapacitates, perhaps it reforms. Their persistent embarrassment is that they seem to license too much. If the point of punishment were simply to discourage crime, then punishing an innocent person whom the public believed guilty might serve that point as well as punishing the guilty one, and sometimes better, since it would be quicker. Most people regard this conclusion as monstrous, which suggests that the effects of punishment, however important, cannot be the whole of its justification.

An expressive theory offers to supply what is missing. On this view punishment is, before anything else, a form of communication: the community's way of declaring that an act was wrong and that the person who did it is answerable for it. This explains at once why the innocent may not be punished. To condemn someone who did nothing is not an inefficient use of punishment but a lie told in its language. It also explains why we distinguish a fine from a tax, though both take money: the tax carries no message of blame, while the fine carries little else.

The obvious objection is that condemnation can be expressed in words. If the point of a prison sentence is to say that an act was wrong, why not simply say so, in a formal judgement solemnly delivered? The expressive theorist's reply is that words alone are too cheap to carry the message. A declaration that costs the offender nothing would be heard as a declaration that the wrong did not matter much; only something that is felt as a burden registers the seriousness of what was done.

This reply is ingenious, but it has an uncomfortable implication. If hard treatment communicates because of the meanings a society attaches to it, then how much hard treatment is needed depends on those meanings, and meanings can drift. A society accustomed to long sentences will hear a short one as a shrug; the same sentence elsewhere might sound like thunder. The expressive theory, in other words, cannot by itself tell us how severe punishment should be, because severity is fixed by convention, and conventions can escalate without limit. That is not a reason to abandon the theory, which captures something the forward-looking view plainly misses. It is a reason to think that the theory needs a partner: some independent principle, perhaps of proportion or of humanity, that sets limits the language of condemnation cannot set for itself.

  3. (C)show that forward-looking theories are mistaken in assuming that punishment actually deters people from committing crime.
  4. (D)argue that public opinion is an unreliable guide to whether accused persons are guilty or innocent.

Why not (D): The public's belief is part of the hypothetical, not the target of the argument, which concerns theories of punishment.

Common misconception: Taking a detail of the hypothetical (public belief, speed) for its purpose.

Difficulty: The purpose is stated in the sentences before and after the case. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-028 · Political philosophy · passage Medium · 440 words · 4 questions

The defence of meritocracy rests on a simple and appealing idea: that positions and rewards should go to those who earn them, through talent and effort, rather than to those born into the right family. Against the background of aristocratic privilege this was a revolutionary thought, and it remains a powerful one. Yet it has been attacked, with increasing force, on the ground that talent and effort are themselves largely matters of luck. No one chooses the abilities they are born with. And the capacity to work hard, to persist through failure and to defer gratification, is shaped by upbringing, health and circumstance in ways that no one controls. If this is so, the critic concludes, nobody deserves the rewards that meritocracy hands out, and the language of merit is a flattering fiction.

The premises of this argument are difficult to deny, but its conclusion moves too fast. It runs together two different claims that a person might make about a reward. One is that she deserves it in a deep moral sense, that the world would be unjust if she did not have it. The other is that she is entitled to it, because she has met the conditions that a fair set of rules laid down in advance. A runner who wins a race under fair conditions is entitled to the prize even if her speed owes much to her genes and her coaching; to take the prize away on the ground that she was lucky would itself be a wrong, because she trained and competed on the understanding that winning would bring it. Entitlement of this kind does not depend on the winner being the author of her own talents. It depends only on the fairness of the rules and her compliance with them.

Seen in this light, a society can reward productive contributions, and honour the expectations its rules create, without pretending that the rewards measure the moral worth of those who receive them. It also leaves open, as it should, the question of whether the rules themselves are fair, including how much inequality of reward they ought to permit. What the luck argument undermines is not the practice of rewarding achievement but a particular story told about it, the story in which the successful have simply earned their place and the unsuccessful have simply failed to earn theirs. That story is corrosive. It encourages the winners to regard their advantages as wholly their own, and it adds to the losers' material disadvantage the further injury of being told that they deserved it. The remedy is not to abolish merit but to hold it more lightly.

CAT-VARC-P01-028-Q1 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ

  3. (C)stop using tests of talent and effort to decide who receives positions and rewards in a society.
  4. (D)treat success as a matter of pure chance that has nothing whatever to do with a person's effort.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 leaves 'open' how much inequality the rules should permit, so the phrase cannot mean reducing rewards.

Why not (C): The same sentence says 'The remedy is not to abolish merit', which rules this out.

Why not (D): The passage says effort is shaped by luck, not that success is pure chance; it keeps rewarding 'productive contributions'.

Common misconception: Reading the phrase as a call to reduce or abolish rewards.

Difficulty: The phrase is glossed by the contrast drawn earlier in the same paragraph. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-029 · Political philosophy · passage Medium · 445 words · 4 questions

In a society whose members hold different and often incompatible views about the meaning of life and the sources of value, on what basis may some of them use the power of the state to make others comply with laws? One influential answer holds that coercive laws must be justifiable by reasons that all reasonable citizens could accept, whatever their wider convictions. A law defended solely by appeal to a doctrine that many of one's fellow citizens reject, whether that doctrine is religious or secular, treats them as subjects to be managed rather than as equals to be persuaded. The requirement to offer such shared, or public, reasons is usually called the ideal of public reason.

Critics have objected that the ideal asks too much of ordinary citizens. People do not keep their deepest convictions in a compartment separate from their political views; for many, their sense of what justice requires flows directly from a philosophical or religious outlook. To tell such citizens that they may vote, campaign and argue only on grounds detachable from that outlook is to ask them to enter public life as strangers to themselves. Worse, the demand seems to fall unevenly, since those whose convictions already happen to resemble the shared vocabulary are asked to give up nothing.

Much of this objection depends on reading the ideal as a rule about what citizens may say. It is more plausible to read it as a test that laws must pass. On this reading a citizen may give whatever reasons move her, in the street, in the press or in the voting booth, and the ideal is satisfied so long as the law that is eventually enacted can also be supported by reasons that do not depend on any one contested doctrine. The burden then falls most heavily on those who exercise official power, on legislators drafting statutes and judges explaining decisions, rather than on citizens in conversation with one another.

This reading does not remove every difficulty. If a citizen offers public reasons she does not herself find convincing, merely because she knows they are the only reasons that will count, her contribution begins to look less like persuasion and more like strategy. The ideal was meant to express respect for others as fellow reasoners, and insincere argument is a poor vehicle for respect. But this worry, though real, tells against a particular style of advocacy rather than against the test itself. A law can be justified in shared terms even if some of its supporters were moved by other considerations, just as a bridge can be sound though some of those who voted to build it understood nothing of engineering.

CAT-VARC-P01-029-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)citizens whose convictions already resemble the shared vocabulary lose nothing by observing it.
  3. (C)citizens with religious convictions are forbidden from voting on any laws that touch on those convictions.
  4. (D)legislators and judges are held to a much stricter standard of justification than ordinary citizens.

Why not (C): No one in the passage proposes a ban on voting; the critics object to limits on the grounds citizens may give.

Why not (D): This is the author's own reading in paragraph 3, offered as a reply, not the critics' complaint of unevenness.

Common misconception: Confusing the critics' unevenness complaint with the author's later point that the burden falls on officials.

Difficulty: The answer is a close paraphrase of one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-029-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
On the reading of the ideal that the author defends in paragraph 3, the ideal of public reason would be violated if
  1. (A)a judge supported a decision with shared reasons that some citizens nevertheless found unconvincing.
  2. (B)a citizen explained to her neighbours that she voted for a law because of her religious convictions.
  3. (C)a coercive law were enacted that could be defended only by appeal to a doctrine many citizens reject.
  4. (D)most supporters of a law had been moved by considerations other than the shared reasons offered for it.

Why not (A): The ideal asks for reasons all reasonable citizens 'could accept' (paragraph 1), not reasons that actually convince everyone.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says a citizen 'may give whatever reasons move her', including in the voting booth, so this is permitted.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says a law 'can be justified in shared terms even if some of its supporters were moved by other considerations'.

Common misconception: Treating the ideal as a rule on citizens' speech, which is the reading the author rejects.

Difficulty: Requires converting a sufficient condition into its violation and checking three permitted cases. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-030 · Political philosophy · passage Medium · 430 words · 4 questions

The case for deciding by majority is usually made on grounds of fairness rather than wisdom. Majorities are not reliably right, but a procedure that counts each vote equally treats each voter as an equal, and no rival procedure seems to do so as well. There is a second, less often stated part of the case. Majority rule is tolerable to those who lose because losing is expected to be temporary. Today's minority may be tomorrow's majority, since coalitions shift as issues change, and a citizen who is outvoted on one question can reasonably expect to be on the winning side of another. Over time, the losses are spread around.

This second condition does not always hold. When the issues that matter most to people line up with a stable division in the society, by language, region or ancestry, for instance, the same group may find itself outvoted on question after question for generations. Its members cast their votes, and their votes are counted equally, but the counting never changes the result. Formal equality at the ballot box then coexists with something very like permanent exclusion. To tell such a minority that it has been treated fairly because each of its votes weighed as much as anyone else's is to mistake the form of fairness for its substance.

Various remedies have been proposed: requirements of supermajorities for certain decisions, the devolution of some powers to regions where the minority is concentrated, electoral systems that give smaller groups a share of seats, and constitutional rights that no majority may override. Each has costs. Supermajority rules protect minorities by making change difficult, which also protects whatever injustices the status quo contains. Devolution can create new minorities within the regions it empowers. Entrenched rights shift power from elected bodies to courts, whose members the minority usually has no means of holding to account.

None of these costs settles the matter, but together they suggest that the problem should be framed differently. The persistent minority is not evidence that majority rule is unjust in itself. It is evidence that one of the conditions under which majority rule is fair has failed. The aim of a remedy, accordingly, should be to restore that condition where possible, for example by encouraging cross-cutting associations that loosen the grip of a single division on political life, rather than to replace majority rule with a different principle of decision. Where the condition cannot be restored, stronger protections may be needed, but they should be understood as repairs to a damaged procedure, not as a rival theory of legitimacy.

  2. (B)majorities are more likely than minorities to reach the correct answer on most questions of public policy.
  3. (C)counting every vote equally is the only procedure that can treat each voter as the equal of every other.
  4. (D)minorities can always appeal to independent courts to protect the interests that matter most to them.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says 'Majorities are not reliably right', and the case is made on grounds of fairness, not wisdom.

Why not (C): This is the first part of the case, and it is hedged: 'no rival procedure seems to do so as well', not that none can.

Why not (D): Courts appear only in paragraph 3, as one remedy with costs, not as part of the case for majority rule.

Common misconception: Choosing the first part of the case, which the stem's 'second' rules out.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in two consecutive sentences. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-031 · Political philosophy · passage Difficult · 604 words · 5 questions

For much of modern political thought, to be free has meant to be left alone. On this view a person's liberty is diminished whenever someone interferes with her choices, by force, by threat or by the closing off of options, and it is enlarged whenever such interference is removed. The view has the merit of simplicity, and it gives a clear direction to political argument: other things being equal, less interference means more freedom. An older tradition, recently revived, holds that this account misses something essential. What makes a person unfree, it says, is not interference as such but dependence on the will of another who could interfere at pleasure, whether or not he ever does.

The point is easiest to see in a case where the two accounts come apart. Imagine a tenant whose lease allows the landlord to evict her at any time and for any reason, and whose landlord happens to be a mild man who has never troubled anyone. On the non-interference view the tenant is perfectly free, since nobody is stopping her from doing anything. Yet she knows her position. She will think twice before complaining about a broken boiler, she will keep her opinion of the landlord to herself, and she may find herself anticipating his preferences in matters he has never raised. The absence of interference in her life is not evidence that she is free; it may be, in part, an effect of her unfreedom, since a person who must stay in another's good graces learns to make interference unnecessary. The rival tradition calls her condition domination, and it defines freedom as the absence of domination.

The shift has striking consequences. Interference, on this view, does not always reduce freedom. A law that restrains me, but that I and others like me can contest through courts, elections and other channels that force those who make it to take our interests into account, does not subject me to anyone's arbitrary will, and so does not make me less free, even though it limits what I may do. Conversely, a benevolent ruler who chooses to interfere very little may still leave his subjects unfree, because what they are permitted to do depends on his continued goodwill.

Critics reply that the notion of arbitrary power is too elastic to do the work asked of it. Almost every relationship, from employment to marriage to the ordinary dealings of neighbours, contains some capacity of one party to make the other's life worse. If all such capacities count as domination, then domination is everywhere and the concept loses its edge; if only some do, we need a criterion, and the obvious candidates seem to smuggle back the very judgements about interference that the theory hoped to avoid. Defenders answer that power is arbitrary when it is not forced to track the interests of those subject to it, and that whether it is so forced is a question about institutions: about rights of exit, of appeal and of collective voice.

This answer is better than the critics allow, but it is not quite enough to turn non-domination into a measure. It tells us where to look rather than how much freedom we will find there. That may be its real value. The non-interference view, by counting acts, trains attention on what happens; the rival view, by asking about standing, trains it on what could happen, and on the structures that make some things unthinkable to attempt. Even if it cannot yield a neat scale, the second view identifies a kind of unfreedom that a tally of interferences would never register.

CAT-VARC-P01-031-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)She shapes her own conduct to stay in the landlord's good graces, so he rarely has any reason to intervene.
  3. (C)Her lease forbids the landlord from interfering with the choices she makes about how to live in the flat.
  4. (D)She is able to contest the landlord's decisions in court whenever he acts against her interests.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Why not (A): No such law is mentioned; the tenant 'will think twice before complaining about a broken boiler' precisely because she lacks protection.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the opposite: the lease 'allows the landlord to evict her at any time and for any reason'.

Why not (D): Contestability is what the passage says makes a law compatible with freedom (paragraph 3); the tenant has no such recourse, since her lease lets the landlord evict her 'at any time and for any reason'.

Common misconception: Assuming that little interference must mean some protection is in place.

CAT-VARC-P01-032 · Political philosophy · passage Difficult · 595 words · 5 questions

Egalitarians hold that there is something wrong with inequality as such: that when some people are much worse off than others through no fault of their own, the situation is bad, and bad in part simply because of the gap. The most famous objection to this view asks us to consider what follows from it. Suppose that the only way to remove an inequality were to make the better off worse off, with no benefit to anyone, as if a town in which some households had gardens and others had none could reach equality only by paving over every garden. If inequality is bad in itself, then such a change would remove something bad, and so would be in one respect an improvement. Yet the change helps no one and harms many. To call it an improvement in any respect, the objectors say, is absurd.

Many philosophers have been moved by this objection to give up equality as a fundamental value. Some have adopted the view that benefits matter more the worse off their recipient is, so that a gain to a poor person counts for more than the same gain to a rich one; others hold that what matters is that everyone should have enough, after which differences cease to be of moral concern. Neither view is troubled by levelling down, since on neither does reducing the well-being of the better off, by itself, improve anything. Both also capture much of what drew people to equality in the first place, which was a concern for those who are badly off.

Yet the objection is less decisive than it is usually taken to be. The egalitarian need not claim that a levelled-down world is better all things considered, only that it is better in one respect while much worse in others. Plural values often pull apart in this way. We can think that a promise kept at terrible cost was in one respect admirable without thinking that it should have been kept. Seen this way, the levelling-down case shows only that equality is not the only thing that matters, which almost no egalitarian ever believed.

This defence succeeds, but at a price. It keeps equality as a value only by making it a strangely impersonal one: something that can make a state of affairs better while making it better for no one at all. Many will find such a value hard to care about. There is, however, another way to understand what egalitarians were after. On a relational view, the core of equality lies not in how goods are distributed but in how people stand to one another: in the absence of rank, servility and contempt, in a society where no one must bow and no one may command another merely by virtue of birth or wealth. Inequalities of resources matter on this view chiefly because, beyond some point, they turn into inequalities of standing, as when wealth buys influence over others or poverty forces deference to them.

The relational view is untouched by the levelling-down objection, because paving over gardens neither creates nor abolishes any relation of rank between neighbours. Whether it can say enough about distribution is a further question. A society might abolish titles and deference while leaving vast differences in income intact, and the relational egalitarian owes us an account of when such differences begin to corrode equal standing. But the view has at least located the value of equality in a relation between persons, which is where most of its defenders always felt it to be.

CAT-VARC-P01-032-Q1 · Inference · MediumMCQ

  2. (B)deny that people who are badly off have any special claim on the resources that a society has to distribute.
  3. (C)treat inequality as bad in itself, but as much less bad than the suffering of people who lack enough to live on.

Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Why not (A): The sufficiency view holds that once everyone has enough, 'differences cease to be of moral concern', so it requires nothing of the kind.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says both views 'capture much of what drew people to equality', namely 'a concern for those who are badly off', which is the reverse.

Why not (C): The views were adopted by those who 'give up equality as a fundamental value', so they do not treat inequality as bad in itself.

Common misconception: Assuming that any view sympathetic to the badly off must still value equality as such.

Difficulty: The answer paraphrases one sentence, but two wrong options misdescribe the views in plausible ways. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-032-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely agree that a relational egalitarian, considering a society that has abolished titles but retains very large differences in wealth, should
  1. (A)set aside questions of income entirely, since the relational view is concerned with standing and not distribution.
  2. (B)regard that society as fully equal in the relevant sense, since no one in it holds any formal rank.

Why not (C): This is levelling down, which the relational view does not need; paragraph 5 says paving gardens 'neither creates nor abolishes any relation of rank'.

Difficulty: One wrong option rests on a half-true reading of paragraph 4's opening; rejecting it needs the qualification at the paragraph's end and the open question in paragraph 5. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P01-032-Q5 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ

CAT-VARC-P01-033 · Applied ethics · passage Easy · 454 words · 4 questions

The case for monitoring employees at work usually rests on two claims. The first is that the employer is paying for the worker's time and is therefore entitled to know how that time is spent. The second is that the worker has agreed to the arrangement: the monitoring policy was set out in the contract, and nobody was forced to sign it. Both claims sound reasonable, and both, I think, prove much less than their defenders suppose.

Take the argument from payment first. An employer buys a worker's labour, not the worker; the wage secures a certain kind of effort over a certain period, and it has never been understood to include a right to observe everything a person does while that effort is being given. A firm that pays a carpenter by the day may inspect the cabinets she builds, but it does not thereby acquire a claim to read her letters during lunch. The argument from consent fares little better. Agreement given by someone who needs the job, in a market where most comparable employers impose similar terms, tells us that the terms were accepted, not that they were fair. Consent of this kind can settle a dispute about what was agreed; it cannot settle whether the agreement should have been offered.

The deeper difficulty is what continuous monitoring does to work itself. When every keystroke and pause is recorded, the employee learns to perform for the record rather than for the task. A support worker whose calls are timed will cut short a conversation with a confused customer even when a longer one would solve the problem; an office worker whose idle minutes are logged will keep the screen busy instead of stopping to think. The record begins to replace the judgement it was supposed to check, and the trust on which most skilled work depends, the expectation that a person will use discretion well when no one is looking, quietly erodes. There is an irony here as well: the information that monitoring yields grows less useful the more thoroughly it is collected, because what it captures is increasingly a display staged for the watcher.

None of this means that employers may never watch. Where a job carries a specific and serious risk, such as handling cash, operating dangerous machinery or opening confidential records, targeted checks are a reasonable response to that risk, and most employees understand them as such. The test I would propose is therefore not whether monitoring was agreed to, but whether it is tied to an identifiable hazard and limited to what that hazard requires. General surveillance, justified only by the employer's wish to know, fails that test, however clearly it has been written into the contract.

Paragraph 1 names the two usual arguments (payment and consent) and says both 'prove much less than their defenders suppose'; paragraph 2 rebuts them, paragraph 3 adds the cost to work itself, and paragraph 4 proposes a test tying monitoring to 'an identifiable hazard'.
