CAT-VARC-P01-034 · Applied ethics · passage Easy · 442 words · 4 questions

Many people who would never tell a lie are perfectly comfortable misleading. Asked whether he has finished a report, an employee replies that he has been working on it all morning, which is true, and which he says precisely so that his manager will conclude that the report is nearly done. He has not lied, he tells himself; if his manager draws the wrong conclusion, that is her inference, not his assertion. The intuition behind this self-defence is widely shared, and it is not merely self-serving. Ordinary moral thinking, many legal systems and a long tradition of moral reasoning have treated the outright falsehood as a graver matter than the true statement artfully deployed.

Yet when we ask what is wrong with lying in the first place, the distinction begins to look thin. The harm of a lie consists chiefly in two things: the listener ends up with a false belief that he did not choose, and the trust he extended to the speaker has been turned against him. Both harms are present in the case of the employee. His manager now believes something false about the report, and she believes it because she assumed, reasonably, that a colleague answering her question was trying to answer it. The employee exploited exactly that assumption. That he did so by selecting true words rather than inventing false ones changes the technique, not the result.

There is, however, one setting in which the distinction earns its keep. In a negotiation over the price of a used car, both parties know that the other is pursuing its own advantage, and each is expected to read the other's statements with suspicion. A seller who says that several people have asked about the car, when several have indeed asked but none has made an offer, leaves the buyer free to discount the remark, and a competent buyer will. Where the listener is on guard by convention, the responsibility for drawing inferences is genuinely shared, and the misleader has exploited much less trust than the liar, who asserts something false that even a wary listener has no means of checking.

What this suggests is that the moral line runs less between lying and misleading than between relationships in which listeners are entitled to rely on a speaker's cooperation and those in which they are not. In the first kind, which includes most of our dealings with doctors, colleagues, friends and family, the careful choice of true but misleading words offers almost no moral shelter. In the second, it offers some. The employee who congratulates himself on never having lied has simply mistaken the setting he is in.

  3. (C)the lie, once discovered, damages the relationship between the speaker and listener beyond repair.
  4. (D)the listener is left with an unchosen false belief, and the trust he placed in the speaker is misused.

CAT-VARC-P01-035 · Applied ethics · passage Medium · 484 words · 4 questions

When a patient can no longer decide for herself, someone else must decide for her, and the question is on what basis. The answer favoured in much of modern medical ethics is that the surrogate, usually a close relative, should choose as the patient would have chosen had she been able to. This standard of substituted judgement has an obvious appeal. It extends the patient's autonomy beyond the point at which she can exercise it, so that even when she is unconscious, her values rather than anyone else's continue to govern what is done to her body. The older alternative, that the surrogate should simply choose whatever is in the patient's best interests, looks paternalistic by comparison, since it invites others to put their idea of a good outcome in place of hers.

The appeal is real, but it depends on there being something for the surrogate to reconstruct. Sometimes there is. A patient who said repeatedly, while healthy, that she would never want to be kept alive on a ventilator has left a clear instruction, and a daughter who honours it is doing exactly what the standard asks. Far more often, however, the situation that arises is one the patient never considered. She may have had firm views about dying at home but none about a trial of dialysis that offers a modest chance of recovery and a large chance of prolonged discomfort. To ask what she would have chosen here is to ask a counterfactual question to which there may be no fact of the matter. The surrogate who answers it confidently is not discovering the patient's will; he is extrapolating from scraps, and the extrapolation is shaped, inevitably, by his own hopes and fears.

This would matter less if the fiction were harmless, but it has two costs. The first is that it hides the surrogate's own judgement behind the patient's name, so that a decision which ought to be open to discussion, with doctors and with other relatives, is presented as beyond discussion: this is simply what she would have wanted. The second is subtler. Families often find substituted judgement consoling precisely because it seems to relieve them of responsibility, and that consolation is not to be despised. But a consolation bought by pretending is fragile, and relatives who later doubt that they read the patient correctly may suffer more than they would have if they had been told, from the start, that they were being asked to judge what was best for her.

The remedy is not to abandon substituted judgement but to confine it to cases where the patient's own wishes are actually known and actually bear on the decision, and to say plainly, in all other cases, that the surrogate is choosing on the patient's behalf what seems best for her. Honesty about which standard is in play is itself a form of respect for the patient.

CAT-VARC-P01-036 · Applied ethics · passage Medium · 479 words · 4 questions

Few practices attract scorn as readily as offsetting. A traveller who pays a fee to have trees planted in compensation for the emissions of her flight is compared, almost by reflex, to the sinner who once bought a pardon: she has not stopped doing the harmful thing, the critic says, she has merely paid to feel better about it. A sharper version of the objection imagines a man who wishes to be unfaithful to his wife and pays a friend to remain faithful to his own, so that the total quantity of fidelity in the world is unchanged. If that arrangement is absurd, and it plainly is, why should paying for trees be any different?

The comparison is instructive, but not in the way the critic intends. Infidelity wrongs a particular person, and the wrong consists in what is done to her; another couple's faithfulness does nothing to repair it, because she was never interested in the global supply of fidelity. The harm of greenhouse emissions has an entirely different structure. It does not fall on anyone in particular by virtue of a relationship with the emitter; it arises from the total concentration of gases in the atmosphere, to which every tonne contributes equally regardless of where it came from. A tonne removed in one place cancels a tonne added in another as far as the eventual damage is concerned. Where a harm is aggregate and interchangeable in this way, compensation elsewhere is not a moral sleight of hand. It is, at least in principle, a genuine undoing.

What the fidelity case shows, then, is that offsetting is incoherent for directed wrongs and coherent for aggregate ones. One cannot offset an insult to a colleague by being kind to a stranger, but one can offset a contribution to a shared stock of harm by subtracting an equal amount from it. The serious questions about carbon offsetting are therefore not philosophical but practical: whether the trees would have been planted anyway, whether they will survive long enough to hold the carbon they are credited with, whether the accounts of the scheme can be trusted. An offset that fails these tests is not a pardon; it is simply a fraud, and the right response to it is an audit, not moral disdain.

I do not want to claim that nothing remains of the critic's unease. A person who comes to regard every harm as purchasable may lose the habit of asking whether she should be doing the harmful thing at all, and a culture of easy offsetting may slow the changes in conduct that will ultimately be needed. These are real worries. But they concern the character of the offsetter and the politics of reform, not the question of whether the harm itself has been undone, and they should not be dressed up as proof that offsetting is a confidence trick.

CAT-VARC-P01-036-Q1 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
As used in paragraph 3, 'directed wrongs' are best understood as wrongs that
  1. (A)consist in what is done to some particular person, so that benefiting someone else cannot repair them.
  2. (B)are committed deliberately and with full knowledge, as opposed to harms that are caused by accident or neglect.
  3. (C)are ordered by one person but carried out by another, as when the unfaithful man pays his friend.
  4. (D)cause damage only through the total amount of similar harm that everyone together has committed.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says infidelity 'wrongs a particular person, and the wrong consists in what is done to her', which is why another couple's faithfulness cannot repair it. Paragraph 3 names such cases 'directed wrongs' and gives a further example: an insult to a colleague cannot be offset by kindness to a stranger.

Why not (B): Intention is never discussed; the contrast in paragraph 3 is with 'aggregate' harms, not with accidental ones.

Why not (C): The friend in paragraph 1 is paid to be faithful, not to commit a wrong; 'directed' refers to the target of the wrong, not to who gives orders.

Why not (D): This describes the aggregate harms that paragraph 3 sets against directed wrongs, so it reverses the meaning.

Common misconception: Reading 'directed' in an everyday sense (deliberate, or ordered) instead of the sense fixed by the contrast with aggregate harms.

Difficulty: The contrast with 'aggregate ones' in the same sentence fixes the meaning. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-036-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ

CAT-VARC-P01-036-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ

  2. (B)Most people who hear both the fidelity case and the author's reply to it continue to find the original objection to offsetting by far the more persuasive of the two.

Why not (B): How persuasive people find an argument does not show that it is unsound; the finding leaves the structure of the harm untouched.

Why not (D): This is the worry about the offsetter's conduct that paragraph 4 already concedes; it bears on character and reform, not on whether a given tonne is cancelled.

Common misconception: Choosing a fact that discredits offsetting in practice, which the author has already separated from the question of principle.

CAT-VARC-P01-037 · Applied ethics · passage Medium · 448 words · 4 questions

Arguments about charity tend to divide into two camps. On one side are those who hold that money given to help others should go wherever it will do the most good, which, given the vast differences in what a sum can buy in different places, often means far from home. On the other are those who insist that we owe something special to our own: our town, our region, our country. The first camp accuses the second of parochialism dressed up as loyalty; the second accuses the first of a cold arithmetic that would dissolve every bond that makes moral life recognisable. Each has hold of something, and each, I think, misidentifies what it is.

Begin with what the defenders of local giving get right. There really are special obligations. A parent owes more to her own children than to children in general, and no plausible ethics of giving asks her to fund a stranger's schooling before her son's. We also owe more to those we have harmed, and to those with whom we share institutions that we sustain and from which we benefit, such as a school we attended or a cooperative to which we belong. These obligations do not arise from distance. They arise from relationships: from dependence, from history, from reciprocity.

The trouble is that the case for local giving usually trades on these relationships while asking for something else. The resident who prefers to give to a shelter in his own city rather than to a health programme abroad rarely stands in any special relation to the people in the shelter. He has not harmed them, does not depend on them and shares no institution with them beyond the accident of living within the same municipal boundary. What draws him is proximity, and proximity by itself has little moral force. A sick stranger in the next street does not matter more than a sick stranger in another country simply because she is closer, though I may be better placed to help her.

That last qualification points to the strongest argument for local giving, which is not about loyalty at all but about knowledge. Near home, donors can see whether their money is used well, and can withdraw it if it is not. This is a serious consideration, but it is an empirical one, and it cuts in both directions: where reliable assessments of distant programmes exist, the advantage of proximity shrinks, and where the local charity is opaque, it disappears. What remains, once relationships and knowledge are set aside, is often a preference for help whose results one can watch, a preference that is understandable but that should not be mistaken for an obligation.

  4. (D)sharing with others an institution that one sustains and benefits from.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says 'We also owe more to those we have harmed', so this is presented as a source.

Common misconception: Assuming that a shared city is a shared institution, which paragraph 3 explicitly denies.

Difficulty: The three sources are listed together in paragraph 2; the exception is marked as an 'accident' in paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-038 · Applied ethics · passage Medium · 459 words · 4 questions

Whether insects can suffer is a question on which serious people disagree, and on which the evidence is likely to remain inconclusive for a long time. Their nervous systems are tiny and organised very differently from ours; some of their behaviour looks like nothing more than reflex, while some looks uncomfortably like the behaviour that, in a dog or a bird, we would take without hesitation as a sign of pain. Faced with this uncertainty, the usual response is to treat it as permission. If we cannot know that insects feel anything, the thought runs, we need not worry about how we treat them.

This inference is much weaker than it looks. Uncertainty about whether a being can suffer is not the same as confidence that it cannot, and in other contexts we do not treat it so. A builder who is unsure whether a house is occupied does not demolish it on the grounds that no one has proved anyone is inside. What uncertainty calls for is a weighing: the probability that harm is occurring, multiplied by how bad the harm would be and by how many beings it would affect. With insects, the last factor is overwhelming. The numbers involved in agriculture, pest control and the growing industry that farms insects for animal feed run to many billions, so that even a modest probability of sentience yields a large expected total of suffering.

The obvious objection is that this style of reasoning proves too much. If any probability of suffering above zero, multiplied by a sufficiently large number of beings, can generate an overriding moral demand, then we might find ourselves obliged to reorganise our lives around bacteria, or around the remote chance that plants feel pain. That objection has force against arguments resting on probabilities so small that they amount to mere logical possibility. It has much less force here. The case for insect sentience is not a bare possibility of the kind that could be raised for any organism; it rests on specific behavioural and physiological features that insects share with animals whose capacity for pain we already accept, even if they share them only partially.

What follows is not that insects should be accorded the same consideration as mammals, nor that every crushed ant is a tragedy. It is that where we deliberately bring insects into existence in vast numbers and kill them, as in insect farming, we should prefer methods of rearing and killing that would minimise suffering if suffering is present, provided the cost of doing so is modest. Precautions of this kind ask little of us. To refuse them on the grounds that sentience has not been proved is to mistake the absence of certainty for the presence of a licence.

CAT-VARC-P01-039 · Applied ethics · passage Difficult · 650 words · 5 questions

Any decision whose costs and benefits are spread over time requires some way of comparing what happens now with what happens later, and the standard device is the discount rate. A benefit expected in a year is counted as worth somewhat less than the same benefit today; one expected in ten years, less again; and so on, at a compounding rate. Within a single human life, and over horizons of a few decades, the practice is largely uncontroversial. Its consequences become startling only when the horizon stretches across centuries. At a rate of five per cent a year, a harm that will occur two hundred years from now is valued today at a tiny fraction of one per cent of its full size, so that a policy which spared our descendants a catastrophe would barely register against even modest present costs. Whether we owe the future anything substantial thus turns, in practice, on a number that is usually set by technical convention.

It helps to separate the reasons that have been given for discounting, because they do not stand or fall together. One is pure time preference: the view that well-being simply matters less the later it occurs. Another appeals to growth. If future people will be considerably richer than we are, then a given sum of money, or a given quantity of goods, will make less difference to their lives than it would to ours, much as a rupee means less to a wealthy person than to a poor one. A third appeals to uncertainty: the further away an outcome, the less sure we can be that it will happen at all, or that there will be anyone for it to happen to.

Pure time preference is the weakest of these, at least when it is applied across generations. A person who prefers a pleasure today to a larger one next year may be imprudent, but the impatience is his own and so are its costs. To discount the well-being of people not yet born merely because they will be born later is a different matter: it is to treat a position in time as a reason for counting someone's suffering as less, in just the way that it would be indefensible to count suffering as less because it occurs in a distant country. The growth argument is more respectable, because it does not deny the equal standing of future people; it says only that the same resources will do them less good. But it is respectable only on its own assumptions. It presupposes that the future will in fact be richer, which is precisely what is in doubt if the harm in question is severe enough to undermine prosperity, and it applies only to losses for which wealth can compensate. A flooded coastline or an extinct species is not made good by a larger economy, and to discount such losses at the rate at which consumption grows is to treat them as though they could be bought back.

The argument from uncertainty deserves more sympathy than critics of discounting usually allow it. It is reasonable to give some weight to the possibility that there will be no future people at all, and a small discount for that possibility is defensible. But the rate this argument supports is far lower than the rates commonly used, and it has nothing to say about harms we are confident will occur. What emerges from these distinctions is that the choice of a discount rate is not the technical matter it is often taken to be. Each component encodes a substantive view: about whether time itself diminishes moral standing, about how confident we are in continued growth, about which losses money can repair. A rate presented as a neutral parameter is, in effect, a verdict on what the present owes to the future, reached without the argument such a verdict requires.

  3. (C)the same resources will do less good for future people if they turn out to be richer than we are.
  4. (D)the well-being of future people matters less than ours simply because it will occur at a later date.

Answer: (C)

Why not (A): The growth argument concerns how much difference a sum makes to richer people; paragraph 3 denies that a larger economy makes good every loss.

Why not (B): This is the third reason, the appeal to uncertainty, described at the end of paragraph 2.

Why not (D): This is pure time preference, which paragraph 2 lists as a separate reason and paragraph 3 rejects across generations.

Common misconception: Confusing the three rationales, which the passage lists one after another.

Difficulty: The definition is given in two sentences; wrong options are the other two rationales. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-039-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Suppose analysts assess a policy that would prevent the permanent loss of a stretch of inhabited coastline two centuries from now, and they discount that loss at the expected rate of growth in consumption. On the passage's reasoning, the most fitting objection is that
  1. (A)the loss lies too far in the future for its size to be estimated with enough confidence for it to enter any calculation at all.
  2. (B)the growth rate hides a pure time preference within it, and such a preference has no defence across generations.
  3. (C)any discount at all is forbidden for harms this far ahead, including the small discount the passage allows for the chance that no future people will exist.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'a small discount for that possibility is defensible', so a blanket ban on discounting contradicts the author.

Common misconception: Reaching for the author's rejection of pure time preference, which targets a different rationale from the one the analysts use.

Difficulty: The solver must match the case to the correct one of three rationales and to the specific limit placed on it; two wrong options use real claims from the passage aimed at the wrong target. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P01-040 · Applied ethics · passage Difficult · 582 words · 5 questions

When a child falls into a disused well or a group of miners is trapped underground, communities will spend almost without limit to bring them out. Engineers are flown in, machines are diverted from other work, and no one asks whether the money might have been better used. The same communities, meanwhile, routinely decline measures that would save far more lives at far lower cost: safer road junctions, better lighting on rural highways, routine screening for common diseases. To many economists and policy analysts, the contrast is simply an error. A life saved by a guardrail is no less a life than one saved by a rescue shaft; that we cannot name the person in advance makes no difference to the value of what is saved. On this view the preference for identified over statistical lives is a bias, and a costly one, to be corrected by accounting that treats every life saved alike.

Much of this critique seems to me correct. The people whose deaths a guardrail prevents are not abstractions; they are as real as the trapped miners, and they would have suffered and been mourned in the same way. If we spend a hundred times more per life on rescue than on prevention, a great many people die who need not have, and the fact that we never learn their names does not make their deaths any less our doing. It is worth noticing, too, that the identified lives we rush to save are often identified by chance: they are the victims whose plight happens to be reported, while others in similar danger elsewhere receive nothing, because no one is watching.

Yet the critique moves too quickly when it concludes that the impulse to rescue has no rational content. What a community expresses when it refuses to abandon a known person in peril is a commitment that has value beyond the life in question: an assurance, to everyone, that if they are ever in that position they will not be left to die because the arithmetic was unfavourable. This assurance is a real good, and it is one that statistical accounting, which counts only lives saved, has no column for. It is enjoyed by people who are never in danger, and it shapes how the members of a community regard one another.

Nor need this good conflict with the analyst's preferred standpoint. The strongest case for prevention is made from the position of someone who does not yet know whether he will be a victim: from there, each of us would prefer the policy that most improves our own chances, and that is usually prevention. But someone in that position would also want the assurance that rescue provides, and would be willing to pay something for it. Properly understood, then, the choice made under that uncertainty is not between rescue and prevention but about the proportion in which to buy them, and it is hard to believe that the proportion would resemble our present spending, in which prevention is starved while rescue is limitless.

The lesson is not that rescue should be abandoned in the name of consistency, a policy that would purchase a few more statistical lives at the price of a community in which no one could count on being saved. It is that the scandal lies on the other side of the ledger. We do not rescue too much; we prevent too little, and the impulse to rescue, rightly valued, gives no excuse for that neglect.

  4. (D)those whose rescue costs less per life than the measures that would prevent similar deaths.

CAT-VARC-P01-041 · Science and reasoning · passage Easy · 441 words · 4 questions

Students meeting physics for the first time are often unsettled to learn that the planes in their textbooks have no friction, the pendulums swing without air, and the planets are treated as points. Economics and population biology are worse offenders: their agents calculate perfectly and their populations are infinitely large. A natural response is to say that science, at least in its models, trades truth for convenience, and that a model is a useful fiction whose only virtue is that it lets us calculate. On this view the idealisation is a debt to be repaid later, when better methods allow the missing details to be added back.

This response gets the matter backwards. An idealisation is not usually a lapse in description but a claim about relevance. To leave friction out of an account of a stone sliding down a smooth slope is to assert, implicitly, that friction makes no appreciable difference to the feature one is trying to explain, namely the way the stone's speed grows with time. If that assertion is correct, the idealised model is not a fiction about the stone; it is a true statement about what the stone's motion depends on. The model is false about the world in its details and accurate about the structure of dependence, and the second is frequently what we wanted to know. Adding every missing factor would not improve such a model; it would bury the very pattern the model was built to display.

This does not mean that every idealisation is innocent. The claim of irrelevance can be wrong, and it is often wrong in a particular way: an assumption that holds well within one range of conditions fails outside it. Treating air as absent is harmless for a stone dropped from a window and disastrous for a feather, or for a parachutist. The danger lies less in the falsehood itself than in forgetting that it was a bet about relevance, so that a model calibrated in one setting is carried into another where the bet no longer pays.

The sensible test, then, is not whether a model is literally true, since almost none are, but whether its conclusions survive when the idealisation is relaxed. If a result about a population holds whether the population is taken to be infinite or merely very large, the assumption of infinity was doing no harm. If the result vanishes as soon as the assumption is loosened, it was an artefact of the simplification rather than a discovery about the world. Read in this way, the frictionless plane is less an embarrassment than a hypothesis, and like any hypothesis it can be checked.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'useful fiction' view because it is described at length in paragraph 1, without noticing that the author rejects it at the start of paragraph 2.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated plainly at the start of paragraph 2 and the end of paragraph 4; the reported view is clearly rejected. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-042 · Science and reasoning · passage Medium · 432 words · 4 questions

A long tradition in the philosophy of science treats explanation and prediction as two faces of one achievement. To explain why an event happened, on this view, is to show that it could have been expected, given the relevant laws and the conditions that held beforehand; to predict an event is to perform the same derivation before the event rather than after it. The symmetry is attractive because it makes explanation answerable to something. An explanation that could not, even in principle, have served as a prediction would seem to be no more than a story told after the fact. The view also promised a single standard for the whole of science, from astronomy, where eclipses are calculated centuries ahead, to clinical medicine, where a diagnosis is expected both to account for a patient's symptoms and to anticipate their course.

Yet the symmetry fails in both directions. A falling barometer reading is an excellent predictor of a coming storm, and anyone who knows the correlation can forecast the weather with it; but no one supposes that the barometer explains the storm. Both are effects of a drop in atmospheric pressure, and the reading earns its predictive power by riding on that common cause. Conversely, we can often explain what we could never have predicted. A geologist can say why a particular hillside gave way, pointing to saturated soil and a weakened layer of clay, without having been able to say beforehand that it would fail that week rather than in some other year. The explanation is sound even though the forecast was out of reach.

What these cases suggest is that explanation and prediction answer to different demands. A predictor need only be reliably connected with what it predicts; it does not matter how the connection arises. An explanation must identify what the outcome depends on: the factors such that, had they been different, the outcome would have been different too. The barometer fails that test, since a broken barometer would not have prevented the storm; the clay passes it, since firmer clay would have held the slope.

None of this makes prediction irrelevant to explanation. A claim about what an outcome depends on usually implies something about what would happen under other conditions, and checking those implications is how such claims are tested. A theory that explains everything and forbids nothing should make us suspicious. But the test is a check on an explanation, not its substance. To confuse the two is to mistake the evidence that an account is right for the thing that makes it an account at all.

CAT-VARC-P01-043 · Science and reasoning · passage Medium · 425 words · 4 questions

The sceptical argument about induction, usually traced to David Hume, has a disarming simplicity. Every inference from observed cases to unobserved ones assumes that the future will resemble the past, or more generally that nature is uniform. That assumption cannot be proved by logic, since its denial involves no contradiction, and it cannot be supported by experience without circularity, since the support would itself be an inference from observed cases. Philosophers have spent a long time looking for a way out, usually by searching for a single principle that would license inductive inference in general.

The search may be misconceived, because there is no such thing as inductive inference in general. Consider two investigators. One measures the melting point of a newly prepared sample of a pure compound and concludes, from a single careful trial, that every sample of that compound will melt at the same temperature. Another measures the height of one adult and draws no conclusion at all about the heights of adults. Both are reasoning well, and they differ not because one has found the universal principle and the other has not, but because each knows something about the kind of thing being studied: that pure compounds are uniform in their melting points, and that people are not uniform in their heights.

On this picture, what licenses an inductive step is not a principle of uniformity but particular facts about particular domains, which tell us how far a sample can be trusted to stand in for the whole. The obvious objection is that this merely relocates the problem. Our confidence that compounds are uniform in their melting points was itself acquired by induction, and so the sceptic can ask what licenses that.

The objection is fair, but it is less damaging than it looks, because it trades on an ambiguity between justifying each belief and justifying all of them together. The regress it describes is real, yet it never requires us to justify all our inductive habits at once. At any moment we lean on some background knowledge in order to test some other part of it, and any item can in turn be questioned while the rest is held fixed. This does not answer the sceptic on the sceptic's own terms, which demand a foundation lying outside experience altogether. It does show that the demand is not one that science, as actually practised, needs to meet in order to be rational, any more than a household needs to audit every account on the same afternoon in order to keep honest books.

CAT-VARC-P01-043-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The passage suggests that the investigator who measured one adult's height drew no general conclusion because
  1. (A)a single measurement of any quantity, however carefully it is taken, is never enough to support a general conclusion.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says each investigator 'knows something about the kind of thing being studied', namely 'that people are not uniform in their heights'. Paragraph 3 adds that such facts tell us 'how far a sample can be trusted to stand in for the whole'.

Why not (C): The passage says nothing about difficulty of measurement; the difference lies in what each investigator knows about the uniformity of the domain.

Which of the following best expresses the main point of the passage?
  1. (A)Because every inductive step depends on earlier inductions, scientific knowledge rests in the end on nothing more than habit.
  2. (B)A single careful trial is more trustworthy than a large sample whenever the substance under study is chemically pure.
  3. (C)Inductive steps are licensed by knowledge of particular domains, and the regress this creates does not make scientific practice irrational.
  4. (D)The sceptical argument about induction fails, since a principle of uniformity can be supported by the record of past successful inferences.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 argues that 'there is no such thing as inductive inference in general', paragraph 3 says inductive steps are licensed by 'particular facts about particular domains', and paragraph 4 grants the regress but argues science need not meet the sceptic's demand 'in order to be rational'.

Why not (A): The passage admits the regress but argues it is 'less damaging than it looks' and does not prevent science from being rational, so this reverses its conclusion.

Why not (B): This inflates one illustration in paragraph 2 into a general rule and leaves out both the domain-based thesis and the reply to the regress.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 explains that support from experience would be circular, and paragraph 4 says the author's view 'does not answer the sceptic on the sceptic's own terms'.

CAT-VARC-P01-043-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following reasoners proceeds in the way the passage regards as sound?
  1. (A)A pollster who simply assumes that nature is uniform concludes that every future election will go the way the last one did.
  2. (B)A botanist, knowing that a certain trait hardly varies within a plant species, judges a new population's trait from a handful of specimens.
  3. (C)A physician who tests a new drug on one patient and, finding it effective, prescribes it to all patients alike.
  4. (D)A geologist who refuses to generalise from any finite set of rock samples, on the ground that induction can never be justified.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The botanist generalises from few cases because of background knowledge that the trait is uniform within the species, which is the pattern of the melting-point investigator in paragraph 2: a sample is trusted to the extent that facts about the domain allow.

Why not (A): This rests on a general principle of uniformity, which paragraph 3 says is not what licenses an inductive step.

Why not (C): Generalising from one case is sound only where the domain is known to be uniform, and nothing suggests patients respond uniformly; this resembles drawing conclusions from one adult's height.

Why not (D): The passage says the regress 'never requires us to justify all our inductive habits at once'; refusing all generalisation is the sceptical paralysis it argues against.

Common misconception: Choosing the physician because a single-case generalisation also appears in the passage, while ignoring that it was licensed only by knowledge of the domain.

Difficulty: Requires transferring the domain-knowledge condition to new cases, one of which copies the surface form of the passage's example. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-043-Q4 · Except / not supported · DifficultMCQ

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says of the melting-point and height investigators, 'Both are reasoning well', though one generalises from a single trial and the other draws no conclusion at all.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 raises this point, and paragraph 4 calls the objection 'fair', so the author accepts it.

CAT-VARC-P01-044 · Science and reasoning · passage Difficult · 481 words · 4 questions

Faced with two theories that fit the evidence equally well, scientists usually prefer the simpler, and they rarely feel the need to say why. When pressed, they tend to offer one of two answers. The first is that simplicity is a convenience: simpler theories are easier to state, to teach and to calculate with, and so they deserve priority in the way that a tidy desk deserves priority over a cluttered one, without any suggestion that tidiness is truer. The second is that nature itself is simple, so that a simple theory is more likely to have captured how things are. Neither answer is satisfactory. The first explains why we might enjoy simple theories but not why we should believe them, and the second asserts, with no evidence that could be independent of the very preference in question, exactly what needed to be shown.

A more promising answer comes from an unglamorous source, the practice of fitting curves to data. Any finite set of measurements, each carrying some error, can be fitted perfectly by a sufficiently complicated curve, one that bends to pass through every point. Such a curve is usually a worse guide to the next measurement than a smoother one that passes near the points without touching them all, because much of what it has faithfully reproduced is noise. Additional adjustable parameters let a model accommodate the accidents of a particular sample, and the price of that accommodation is paid when the model meets new data. Seen in this light, a preference for simplicity is a policy for estimating well from limited and imperfect evidence, not a belief about the architecture of the world.

This answer has a cost that its admirers do not always acknowledge. If simplicity earns its keep by protecting against noise, then its value should shrink as data become more abundant and more precise, and statistical practice broadly bears this out: with enough clean data, more complex models often predict better, because they can capture real structure that a simple model would smooth away. Simplicity, on this account, is not a standing virtue but a discount applied in proportion to our ignorance. Nor is it clear that simplicity can even be measured independently of how a theory is written. Counting parameters seems objective until one notices that a relation expressed compactly in one set of variables may require several parameters when it is rewritten in another.

None of this returns us to the view that simplicity is mere taste. The statistical argument shows that, in the situations scientists usually face, favouring the simpler of two well-fitting hypotheses is a reliable way to be less wrong. What it does not show is that the simpler hypothesis is, for that reason, more likely to be true; and the habit of speaking as though it did has lent the principle an authority it borrowed rather than earned.

CAT-VARC-P01-044-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a curve that passes exactly through every measured point is usually a poor guide to the next measurement because

  2. (B)complicated curves are harder to calculate with, so that mistakes creep in when they are used to predict.
  3. (C)nature is simple, so a complicated curve cannot correspond to the way things really are.
  4. (D)such a curve can be drawn only when the measurements are entirely free of error.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says such a curve is a worse guide 'because much of what it has faithfully reproduced is noise'.

Why not (B): Ease of calculation belongs to the 'convenience' answer of paragraph 1; paragraph 2 explains the poor prediction by noise, not by calculating mistakes.

Why not (C): This is the second answer in paragraph 1, which the author calls unsatisfactory; paragraph 2 is offered precisely as an alternative to it.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says any set of measurements 'each carrying some error' can be fitted perfectly, so the option contradicts it.

Common misconception: Carrying over one of the two rejected answers from paragraph 1 instead of reading the reason given in paragraph 2.

Difficulty: The reason is stated directly in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-044-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim that simplicity is 'a discount applied in proportion to our ignorance'?

CAT-VARC-P01-045 · Science and reasoning · passage Easy · 413 words · 4 questions

It is often said that a scientific theory can never be proved but can always be refuted: no number of successful predictions establishes it, while a single failed one brings it down. The slogan captures something real about the asymmetry between confirmation and refutation, but as a description of how theories should be treated it is too simple. A prediction is never derived from a theory alone. It depends also on assumptions about the instruments used, the conditions of the experiment and the other bodies or forces that might be at work. When a prediction fails, logic tells us that something in this whole bundle is wrong; it does not tell us which part.

The history of astronomy offers a well-known illustration. In the first half of the nineteenth century, the observed orbit of Uranus departed from what Newtonian mechanics predicted. Astronomers did not abandon Newton. They supposed instead that an unseen planet was pulling on Uranus, calculated where it would have to be, and in 1846 Neptune was found close to the predicted position. The anomaly, far from refuting the theory, became one of its most celebrated successes.

The same strategy, however, does not always succeed. A small discrepancy in the orbit of Mercury was also attributed to an undiscovered planet, which some astronomers even named, but no such planet was ever found. The discrepancy was finally accounted for only by Einstein's general theory of relativity, which replaced Newton's account of gravitation rather than adding to it. In hindsight, the two anomalies look entirely different. At the time, they looked much the same, and the decision to treat both in the same way was reasonable.

The lesson is not that anomalies should be ignored, nor that theories should be given up at the first sign of trouble. It is that there is no rule which tells us in advance when an anomaly is a puzzle to be solved within a theory and when it is a sign that the theory is failing. Tenacity is a virtue in science, since a theory abandoned at the first difficulty would never have the chance to show what it can do; but tenacity shades into dogma when a theory is protected by one convenient assumption after another, each invented for no reason except to save it. Telling the two apart is a matter of judgement, informed by how many anomalies have accumulated and by whether the assumptions used to explain them away have earned independent support.

CAT-VARC-P01-045-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)the theory itself is false, whatever assumptions were made about the instruments and the conditions of the experiment.
  3. (C)the instruments used in the experiment were faulty or had been wrongly calibrated.
  4. (D)the theory must be replaced by another one that also explains its earlier successes.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1: 'When a prediction fails, logic tells us that something in this whole bundle is wrong; it does not tell us which part.' The bundle includes the theory and the assumptions about instruments, conditions and other forces.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says logic shows only that 'something in this whole bundle is wrong', so it cannot single out the theory.

Why not (C): Instruments are one part of the bundle, but logic 'does not tell us which part' is at fault.

Why not (D): Nothing in paragraph 1 says logic requires replacement; the Uranus case in paragraph 2 shows a theory surviving a failed prediction.

Common misconception: Accepting the refutation slogan of the opening sentence, which the author says is 'too simple'.

Difficulty: The answer is a close paraphrase of the last sentence of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-046 · Science and reasoning · passage Medium · 411 words · 4 questions

A familiar picture of scientific objectivity confines values to the edges of inquiry. Values may legitimately decide which problems are worth studying and how results are used, but in the central act of science, deciding whether the evidence supports a hypothesis, they are supposed to have no place. A researcher who lets personal hopes affect whether a conclusion is accepted is, on this picture, simply doing bad science, and the remedy is a stricter separation between the laboratory and the world outside it.

The trouble is that evidence rarely settles a question outright, and so accepting a hypothesis always involves deciding that the evidence is strong enough. How strong is strong enough? Consider a laboratory testing whether an industrial solvent damages the liver. If it wrongly concludes that the solvent is safe, workers may be harmed; if it wrongly concludes that the solvent is dangerous, a useful product may be withdrawn and money wasted. Which error is worse, and therefore how much evidence to demand before declaring the solvent harmful, is not something the data can decide. It depends on how one weighs injured workers against lost revenue, which is a question of value. Two laboratories examining identical data could therefore set the threshold in different places, and neither would have misread a single measurement.

A natural reply is that scientists need not accept or reject anything. They could report the probability that the hypothesis is true, given the evidence, and leave the decision to those who must act. The reply is attractive but harder to carry out than it sounds. Before any probability can be reported, the investigators must decide how to classify ambiguous tissue samples, which measurements to discard as faulty and which statistical model to fit, and each of these decisions can err in either direction, with the same uneven consequences. The weighing of errors does not disappear; it moves to a stage where it is less visible.

It would be a mistake, however, to conclude that anything goes. The argument shows that values can properly bear on how much evidence to require, given what is at stake if one is wrong. It does not show that values can bear on what the evidence is, or license a researcher to discount an inconvenient result because it is unwelcome. The distinction is between setting the bar and moving the evidence, and the integrity of science depends on keeping the two apart, not on pretending that the bar sets itself.

CAT-VARC-P01-046-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The solvent example implies that a laboratory which judged harm to workers to be far worse than lost revenue should
  1. (A)report only the cost of withdrawing the solvent and leave every judgement about harm to the firm's managers.
  2. (B)demand more evidence of harm than usual before declaring the solvent dangerous to the workers who use it.
  3. (C)be prepared to declare the solvent harmful on less evidence than it would require if both errors seemed equally serious.
  4. (D)refuse to reach any conclusion until the evidence about the solvent leaves no room for any doubt at all.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 says how much evidence to demand 'before declaring the solvent harmful' depends on which of the two errors is worse. If wrongly calling it safe is much worse, the bar for declaring harm should be lower.

Why not (A): The example is about how the weighing of errors sets the evidential threshold; nothing in it suggests reporting costs alone or handing the question to managers.

Why not (B): This reverses the logic: if wrongly calling the solvent safe is the worse error, the laboratory should require less evidence, not more, before declaring harm.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says 'evidence rarely settles a question outright', and the example concerns how much evidence to demand, not waiting for certainty.

Common misconception: Assuming that taking harm seriously always means demanding more evidence, which reverses the direction of the trade-off.

Difficulty: One step of reasoning from the example in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-046-Q3 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ

  2. (B)It concedes that the argument built on the solvent example fails, and restores the picture of objectivity in paragraph 1.

CAT-VARC-P01-046-Q4 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In the final paragraph, 'moving the evidence' refers to
  1. (A)raising or lowering the amount of evidence required in the light of what is at stake if one errs.
  2. (B)transferring the weighing of errors to an earlier stage of the research where it is harder to see.
  3. (C)presenting the evidence to the people who must act instead of accepting or rejecting the hypothesis oneself.
  4. (D)allowing a preference for one conclusion to alter what the results are taken to be, for instance by setting aside an unwelcome finding.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 says values may not 'bear on what the evidence is, or license a researcher to discount an inconvenient result because it is unwelcome', and then labels this 'moving the evidence' as opposed to 'setting the bar'. The choices about ambiguous samples in paragraph 3 are a weighing of errors, part of setting the bar; moving the evidence is letting a wished-for conclusion decide what the results are.

Why not (A): This is 'setting the bar', which the paragraph calls a proper role for values and contrasts with moving the evidence.

Why not (B): This echoes paragraph 3, where the weighing 'moves to a stage where it is less visible'; that describes method choices, not the improper act named in paragraph 4.

Why not (C): This is the proposal in paragraph 3 that scientists report probabilities, not what the final paragraph means by moving the evidence.

Common misconception: Matching the verb 'moves' in paragraph 3 to 'moving' in paragraph 4, though the two describe different things.

Difficulty: A wrong option reuses a near-identical word from the previous paragraph with a different meaning. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-047 · Science and reasoning · passage Medium · 582 words · 5 questions

Thought experiments occupy an awkward place in the theory of knowledge. Scientists and philosophers have long used them to settle questions, and some of the most admired episodes in the history of physics turn on an imagined scenario rather than an actual measurement. Yet a thought experiment, by definition, collects no new data. The experimenter sits still and imagines. If we learn something about the world this way, it is not obvious where the information comes from, and two answers have dominated discussion.

According to the first, a thought experiment is an argument in disguise. Its vivid scenario can always be rewritten as a set of premises and a conclusion, and whatever it teaches, it teaches because the premises were already believed and the conclusion follows from them. The imagery is persuasive packaging, useful perhaps for teaching, but adding nothing to the logical content. According to the second, the best thought experiments give us something no argument could: a kind of direct intellectual insight into the necessary structure of nature, as if the mind could see a law of physics in the way the eye sees a colour.

Neither answer fits the most celebrated example well. Galileo asked his readers to imagine a heavy body and a light one tied together and dropped. If heavier bodies fall faster, as the received physics of his day held, the light body should hold back the heavy one, so that the pair falls more slowly than the heavy body alone; but the pair is heavier than the heavy body, so it should fall faster. The supposition contradicts itself, and the natural way out is that all bodies fall at the same rate, at least in the absence of a resisting medium. The argument reading can reconstruct this, but only by writing in premises that no one had formulated before the scenario was imagined: for instance, that the speed of a composite body must lie somewhere between the speeds of its parts. The insight reading, for its part, makes the result mysterious, as though Galileo had glimpsed a law rather than noticed a contradiction in what he and his readers already believed.

A better account is that thought experiments work by drawing on knowledge we have but have never made explicit. Everyone who has carried a load or watched things fall has a large stock of tacit expectations about how bodies behave. A well-constructed scenario brings these expectations to bear on a case where they collide with an explicit theory, and so exposes a tension that was there all along. What is new is not the information but its arrangement. This explains why thought experiments can be genuinely informative without new data, and also why they cannot be reduced to an argument that someone might have written down beforehand: the premises are found by running the scenario, not before it.

It also explains when they should not be trusted. Our tacit expectations were formed in a narrow band of experience, among objects of moderate size moving at moderate speeds. A thought experiment that asks us to imagine the very small, the very fast or the very distant invites us to apply those expectations where they were never tested, and the resulting conviction, however vivid, carries only the authority of the experience behind it. The method is neither a trick of rhetoric nor a window onto necessity. It is a way of interrogating what we already know, and it is exactly as reliable as that knowledge.

CAT-VARC-P01-047-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best captures the central claim of the passage?
  1. (A)Thought experiments are arguments in disguise, whose vivid imagery adds persuasive force but nothing to the logical content of their conclusions.
  2. (B)The best thought experiments give direct insight into the necessary laws of nature, as Galileo's case of the falling bodies shows.
  3. (C)Thought experiments inform by bringing unstated expectations into conflict with explicit theory, and are only as reliable as those expectations.
  4. (D)Thought experiments should not be trusted in modern physics, since its subject matter lies far outside everyday experience.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 4 says thought experiments draw on 'knowledge we have but have never made explicit', exposing a tension with explicit theory, and paragraph 5 concludes that the method 'is exactly as reliable as that knowledge'. Paragraphs 2 and 3 set up and reject the two rival views.

Why not (A): This is the first of the two views in paragraph 2, and paragraph 4 says thought experiments 'cannot be reduced to an argument that someone might have written down beforehand'.

Why not (B): This is the second view, which paragraph 3 says 'makes the result mysterious'; the author uses Galileo's case against it, not for it.

Why not (D): Paragraph 5 warns about imagined cases of the very small, fast or distant, but the passage does not dismiss a whole field; this inflates one caution into the thesis.

Common misconception: Choosing one of the two views the passage describes at length, or turning the closing caution into the main claim.

Difficulty: The central claim spans two paragraphs and must be separated from two rival views given equal space. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-047-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to paragraph 3, the supposition that heavier bodies fall faster contradicts itself because the two bodies tied together should fall
  1. (A)more slowly than the light body alone, since the air around them drags the heavier of the two bodies back.
  2. (B)more slowly than the heavy body alone, since the light one holds it back, and yet faster, since the pair is heavier.
  3. (C)at exactly the speed of the heavy body alone, since the light body, being so much lighter, adds nothing to the pair.
  4. (D)faster than either body alone in the imagined case, while in actual trials the pair is observed to fall more slowly.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3: 'the light body should hold back the heavy one, so that the pair falls more slowly than the heavy body alone; but the pair is heavier than the heavy body, so it should fall faster'. The same supposition yields both predictions, so it 'contradicts itself'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says the light body holds back the heavy one; air enters only in the closing qualification about 'a resisting medium', not in the contradiction.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says 'the pair is heavier than the heavy body', so the light body does add weight; the contradiction comes from two opposite predictions, not from one equal speed.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says a thought experiment 'collects no new data'; the contradiction in paragraph 3 lies inside the supposition itself, not between imagination and observation.

Common misconception: Thinking the contradiction is between the imagined result and an observed one, when it arises wholly within the supposition.

Difficulty: The two halves of the contradiction are set out in consecutive clauses of paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-048 · Science and reasoning · passage Difficult · 603 words · 5 questions

Statements of chance are among the most familiar in science and ordinary life, and among the hardest to interpret. When we say that a fair coin has a chance of one half of landing heads, what are we saying? The most influential answer identifies chance with frequency: the chance of heads is the proportion of heads in a long run of tosses, or the limit towards which that proportion tends as the run is extended without end. The answer has the great merit of tying chance to something that can be counted, and it explains why observed frequencies are our main evidence about chances.

Its difficulties begin with the single case. A particular coin may be tossed once and then melted down; a particular patient either will or will not recover from a particular operation. Yet we speak readily of the chance that this toss lands heads or that this patient recovers, and such judgements guide real decisions. The frequency view can treat them only by placing the single case in a class of similar cases and borrowing the frequency of the class. And here a second difficulty appears, because every case belongs to many classes. The patient is a man of sixty, a non-smoker, a resident of a particular city, someone treated by a particular surgeon, and the recovery rates in these classes may differ considerably. Which frequency is the patient's chance?

Dissatisfaction with this reference class problem has led many to prefer a propensity view, on which chance is a physical tendency of a particular set-up to produce a given outcome, a property the single toss or the single operation possesses whether or not any long run ever occurs. The propensity view handles the single case gracefully, but at a price. It is unclear what a propensity is, beyond a name for whatever it is that frequencies are evidence of, and the view does not escape the problem it was meant to solve. The tendency of a set-up depends on how the set-up is described: the toss described as a toss of a fair coin has one propensity, while the toss described down to the exact force and angle of the flick may have another, close to certainty.

The persistence of the problem across both views suggests that it is not a defect peculiar to either but a feature of chance itself. A chance is not a property an event has simply, but a property it has relative to a specification of the circumstances. Asking for the chance of an event, full stop, is like asking for the speed of a train without saying relative to what: the question is not unanswerable but incomplete. What remains open, and must be settled by our purposes rather than by the world, is which specification to use. For a gambler who cannot measure the flick, the description 'fair coin' is the relevant one; for a physicist with a high-speed camera, it is not.

Some will feel that this concedes too much, making chance a matter of perspective rather than a feature of the world. But relativity to a description is not the same as subjectivity. Once the specification is fixed, the chance is fixed too, and two observers who adopt the same description and disagree about the chance cannot both be right. Velocity is no less objective for being relative to a frame. What the account gives up is only the idea that there is a single privileged description, the one nature itself would choose, and it is far from clear that anything in science requires such a description.

  2. (B)assigns a definite chance to a single toss even when no long run of tosses ever takes place.
  3. (C)ties chance to something countable and so explains why observed frequencies serve as evidence about chances.
  4. (D)removes the need to decide which class of similar cases a particular event belongs to.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 says the frequency answer 'has the great merit of tying chance to something that can be counted, and it explains why observed frequencies are our main evidence about chances'.

Why not (A): This describes the propensity view of paragraph 3, not the frequency view.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the frequency view's 'difficulties begin with the single case'; handling the single case is the merit claimed for the propensity view.

Why not (D): The need to choose a class is exactly the reference class problem that paragraph 2 raises against the frequency view.

Common misconception: Confusing the merits of the frequency view with those of the propensity view.

Difficulty: The merit is stated in the last sentence of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-049 · Traditions and the good life · passage Easy · 410 words · 4 questions

Governments that once judged their success almost entirely by output have lately begun to ask citizens a different question: all things considered, how satisfied are you with your life these days? The answers, usually given on a scale from zero to ten, are collected, averaged and compared across regions and years, and a rising average is increasingly cited as evidence that policy is working. The appeal of this approach is easy to understand. Income is at best a means to living well, and a measure that asks people directly about their lives seems to cut out the middleman.

Yet the question is less direct than it looks. To answer it, a respondent must do something quite unlike reporting a sensation. She must call to mind her life as a whole, choose the standards by which it is to be judged, and compare the one with the other. The number she gives is therefore an evaluation, and evaluations depend on the standards brought to them. Two people whose days contain the same mixture of pleasure, worry and boredom may give very different scores because one expects more from life than the other. A person who has learned to expect little can report contentment with circumstances that would dismay a neighbour, and a population whose expectations rise quickly can report falling satisfaction even as its conditions improve.

None of this makes the surveys worthless. Evaluations matter in their own right: how people judge their lives shapes what they choose, whom they blame and whether they think change is possible. The scores also move in ways we would expect, dropping when people lose their jobs or fall seriously ill. The error lies not in collecting the numbers but in treating them as a complete reading of how well people are living. A measure of judgement is not a measure of experience, and neither is it a measure of whether a life contains the goods, such as health, friendship and freedom, that most traditions of thought have held to be worth having whatever one happens to feel about them.

A sensible policy, then, would treat satisfaction scores as one instrument among several, read alongside measures of how people actually spend and feel their days and of what they are really able to do. Used alone, the scores reward whatever lowers expectations as readily as whatever improves lives, and a government that steered by them alone could succeed by teaching its citizens to want less.

  3. (C)argue that life-satisfaction scores record expectation-dependent judgements and so should supplement, not replace, other measures of wellbeing.
  4. (D)show that life-satisfaction surveys are unreliable because respondents cannot accurately report how they feel from moment to moment.

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 shows that the score is 'an evaluation' that depends on 'the standards brought to' it, paragraph 3 concedes the surveys have value but warns against 'treating them as a complete reading', and paragraph 4 recommends treating them as 'one instrument among several'. The correct answer states that argument and its scope.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 calls income 'at best a means to living well', and paragraph 4 recommends using the scores 'as one instrument among several', not going back to output.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says answering is 'quite unlike reporting a sensation'; the problem is that the score is an evaluation, not that feelings are misreported, and paragraph 3 says the surveys are not worthless.

Common misconception: Picking the option that sounds most critical of the surveys and missing the concession in paragraph 3.

Difficulty: The thesis and concession are signposted ('Yet', 'None of this makes the surveys worthless'), and the wrong options are clearly too strong or off course. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-050 · Traditions and the good life · passage Medium · 454 words · 4 questions

The handbook of Epictetus's teachings opens with a distinction that has outlived nearly everything else in ancient ethics: some things are up to us and some are not. Our judgements, intentions and desires are up to us; our bodies, our possessions, our reputations and the offices we hold are not. In its modern popular form the distinction is offered as a technique for calm. Worry, it is said, comes from caring about what one cannot change, so the remedy is to stop caring about it, and the person who masters this will be undisturbed by traffic, criticism and bad news alike.

This reading is not exactly wrong, but it inverts the order of the original argument. For the Stoics, tranquillity was a consequence, not the goal. The goal was to place one's good where it actually lay, in the quality of one's own choosing, and the distinction between what is and is not up to us was a claim about value before it was advice about feeling. To say that a friend's illness is not up to me is not to say that I should be indifferent to it; it is to say that my goodness as a person does not depend on whether she recovers, but on whether I act well toward her. The Stoic was expected to visit the sick, serve in public life and keep his promises with full seriousness, while declining to stake his worth on how any of it turned out.

Read this way, the doctrine is more demanding than the popular version, not less. It asks for constant attention to one's own judgements, which the Stoics thought were the real source of most distress, and it offers no permission to withdraw. Someone who uses it to stop voting because elections are not up to him has learned a convenient half of the lesson and discarded the half that mattered.

There is, however, a genuine difficulty that the more careful reading does not remove. The line between what is up to us and what is not is drawn more sharply in the doctrine than in life. Whether I pass an examination is not wholly up to me, but it is hardly independent of my effort; whether my child grows up kind is partly my doing and partly not. Most of what we care about lies in this middle region, and a doctrine that sorts everything into two bins must either treat such outcomes as indifferent, which seems false to how they matter, or admit that our worth is, after all, partly hostage to fortune. The Stoics chose the first answer. One can admire the clarity of that choice and still doubt that it describes a life anyone actually leads.

CAT-VARC-P01-050-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The example of a friend's illness in paragraph 2 serves to
  1. (A)illustrate the popular claim that worry comes from caring about outcomes that one is quite unable to change.
  2. (B)prove that a person's goodness depends partly on whether the people he helps actually recover.
  3. (C)suggest that the Stoics regarded the recovery of friends as a matter of no importance whatever.
  4. (D)show that judging an outcome to be not up to us is compatible with caring about it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 argues that the distinction is a claim about where one's good lies, not a recommendation of indifference. The illness example makes this concrete: the outcome is not up to me, yet I should still 'act well toward her'; only my worth is not staked on her recovery.

Why not (A): The popular reading is set out in paragraph 1 and corrected in paragraph 2; the example belongs to the correction, saying the Stoic is not 'indifferent' to the illness.

Why not (B): The example says goodness 'does not depend on whether she recovers, but on whether I act well toward her', which reverses the option.

Why not (C): The example says the opposite: denying the outcome is up to me 'is not to say that I should be indifferent to it'.

Common misconception: Attaching the example to the popular reading in paragraph 1 instead of the correction in paragraph 2.

CAT-VARC-P01-050-Q2 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following lawyers acts most fully in the spirit of the doctrine as the author interprets it in paragraphs 2 and 3?

  2. (B)One who prepares every case thoroughly, argues it as well as she can, and judges her own conduct by the quality of that work rather than by the verdict.
  3. (C)One who works on each case with great intensity and full seriousness because she believes her worth as a lawyer is measured by how many of her cases she wins.

Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

On the author's reading, the doctrine asks for full engagement (the Stoic serves 'with full seriousness') while locating one's good in 'the quality of one's own choosing' rather than in results. Only the correct answer combines full effort with judging oneself by conduct rather than verdict.

Why not (A): This is the withdrawal the author rejects: the doctrine 'offers no permission to withdraw', and the voter who stops voting has 'discarded the half that mattered'.

Why not (C): She does serve with full seriousness, but she stakes her worth on outcomes, which the Stoic is said to decline: 'declining to stake his worth on how any of it turned out'.

Why not (D): This is the popular version of paragraph 1, stopping caring in order to be calm; paragraph 2 says not being up to me does not mean being 'indifferent'.

CAT-VARC-P01-051 · Traditions and the good life · passage Easy · 434 words · 4 questions

Among the stories collected in the Zhuangzi, one of the best known concerns an enormous tree. A master carpenter passes it without a second glance, and when his apprentice asks why so magnificent a specimen has been ignored, he explains that its wood is good for nothing: boats made from it would sink, coffins would rot and tools would split. That night the tree appears to the carpenter in a dream and rebukes him, pointing out that trees bearing fruit are stripped and broken and never live out their natural years; its own uselessness, it explains, is precisely the reason it has lived so long and grown so large.

The usual moral drawn from the story is a prudent one: in a dangerous world, conspicuous talent invites exploitation, and the wise keep their heads down. That reading has support in the text, which elsewhere praises figures who survive by escaping notice. But it makes the story smaller than it is. The carpenter's mistake is not merely that he underrates the tree; it is that he assumes the only question worth asking about a tree is what it is good for to a carpenter. The tree's reply amounts to the observation that usefulness is always usefulness to someone, for some purpose, and that a thing seen only through that lens is not really seen at all.

This point has lost none of its edge. We are surrounded by institutions that measure things by their contribution to some further end, and we have grown skilled at the carpenter's glance. A subject of study is asked to justify itself by the jobs it leads to, a stretch of land by the revenue it can produce, an hour of a person's time by what it yields. Each of these questions is reasonable on its own, and institutions that never asked them would be failing in their responsibilities. What the story warns against is letting one of them become the only question, so that whatever cannot answer it, a friendship, a landscape, an afternoon of unhurried conversation, is passed by as if it were not there.

It would be a mistake, though, to read the story as a rejection of usefulness as such. Boats and coffins must be made from something, and a world of nothing but magnificent useless trees would be a hard place to live. The story's target is narrower and more interesting: the habit of mind that cannot see a tree as anything other than timber not yet cut, and that mistakes the list of its own purposes for an inventory of what the world contains.

CAT-VARC-P01-051-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the usual moral drawn from the story of the tree is that
  1. (A)every question about what a thing is good for is unreasonable and should be dropped altogether.
  2. (B)things that are of no use to anyone have no value and are rightly passed by without a second glance.
  3. (C)people with conspicuous gifts are likely to be exploited, so wise people avoid drawing attention to themselves.
  4. (D)craftsmen should learn to find uses even for timber that seems useless at first sight.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 states the usual moral: 'in a dangerous world, conspicuous talent invites exploitation, and the wise keep their heads down'. The correct answer paraphrases this.

Why not (A): This is neither the usual moral nor the author's view; paragraph 3 says each such question 'is reasonable on its own'.

Why not (B): That is the carpenter's attitude, which the tree rebukes; the usual moral sides with the tree, recommending that the wise 'keep their heads down'.

Why not (D): Nothing in the passage recommends finding new uses; the story praises the tree for escaping use, and the usual moral concerns survival.

Common misconception: Confusing the carpenter's view, which the story rejects, with the moral readers draw from the story.

Difficulty: The answer is a direct paraphrase of the first sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Why not (B): The carpenter passes the tree 'without a second glance' and misses its value, so the glance is the opposite of careful study.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 uses the phrase figuratively for institutions that 'measure things by their contribution to some further end'; it is not praise of craft expertise.

CAT-VARC-P01-051-Q3 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best expresses the author's main claim about the story of the tree?

  2. (B)Its chief target is a way of seeing that reduces things to their usefulness to someone, not usefulness itself.
  3. (C)It shows that things are valuable in proportion to how little they can be used by people.
  4. (D)It demonstrates that modern institutions are wrong to ask what subjects of study or land are good for.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says the carpenter's real mistake is assuming that 'the only question worth asking about a tree is what it is good for to a carpenter', and paragraph 4 names the target as 'the habit of mind that cannot see a tree as anything other than timber not yet cut', explicitly not usefulness as such.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 grants that this reading 'has support in the text' but says it 'makes the story smaller than it is', so it is not the author's main claim.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 rejects reading the story 'as a rejection of usefulness as such'; nothing ranks things by uselessness.
