CAT-VARC-P01-051-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
Which of the following, if it were added to the end of the story, would most weaken the author's reading of it?
  1. (A)The tree admits it would gladly have become timber had that been safe, and values its uselessness only as a way of staying alive.
  2. (B)On waking, the carpenter tells his dream to the apprentice, who doubts whether a tree could ever really speak to anyone.
  3. (C)The carpenter, waking from his dream, refuses to let his apprentice cut the tree down and reflects at length that he had misjudged it from the start.
  4. (D)Travellers continue to rest in the shade of the great tree for many generations after the carpenter's death.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The author argues that the story is more than prudential advice: it criticises seeing things only by their usefulness. If the tree itself said it valued uselessness only as a survival strategy and would happily have been timber, the story would reduce to the prudent moral that the author says 'makes the story smaller than it is'.

Why not (B): Whether the dream is credible as an event has no bearing on what the story is criticising; the doubt neither confirms nor undermines the author's reading over the prudential one.

Why not (C): A carpenter who comes to see the tree differently fits the author's reading, which concerns his way of seeing; it does not weaken it.

Why not (D): This merely shows the tree surviving and offering another kind of benefit; it is consistent with both the prudential and the author's readings.

Common misconception: Choosing an ending that changes the plot dramatically rather than one that bears on the competing interpretations.

CAT-VARC-P01-052 · Traditions and the good life · passage Medium · 429 words · 4 questions

Epicurus held that death is nothing to us, since where death is, we are not, and his Roman follower Lucretius added an argument that has been discussed ever since. Look back, Lucretius suggests, at the endless stretch of time that passed before you were born, which did not trouble you then and does not trouble you now. The time after your death is a mirror held up to that earlier time, and it would be inconsistent to dread the one while feeling nothing about the other. If non-existence before birth is no evil, non-existence after death can be none either.

The argument has a pleasing economy, and against one target it is decisive. Many people who fear death picture it, half consciously, as a condition they will find themselves in: a darkness, a silence, an interminable wait. Against this picture the mirror works admirably: nobody imagines that they spent the centuries before their birth sitting patiently in the dark, and the comparison exposes the fear of being dead as a confusion, since there will be no one there to suffer the darkness.

But most people who fear death are not chiefly afraid of a condition; they are afraid of losing what they would otherwise have had: the next conversation, the completion of their work, the sight of their children grown. Here the symmetry breaks down, because our attitudes to past and future are not symmetrical even in ordinary life; we look forward to pleasures and dread pains that are still to come in a way that has no counterpart for those already over. More importantly, the goods we stand to lose by dying are goods that this very life would have contained had it continued, whereas the goods of the centuries before our birth were never ours to lose. A person could hardly have been born a hundred years earlier and remained the same person, with the same parents and history; but the same person can very easily die later than he in fact does.

This does not mean that Lucretius has nothing to teach. If the fear of death is partly a fear of a condition, then his argument removes part of the fear, and that is no small gift. What remains is better described as grief in advance for a life cut short, and grief of that kind is not a confusion to be argued away. It is the shadow cast by caring about one's own future, and a consolation that worked perfectly against it would have to work by teaching us to care less.

CAT-VARC-P01-052-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the mirror argument is most effective against
  1. (A)the dread of the physical pain that people expect to suffer in the course of dying.
  2. (B)the fear of missing the future events and achievements that a longer life would have brought.
  3. (C)the fear of death imagined as a condition in which one will somehow find oneself.
  4. (D)the regret people feel about the many centuries of history that passed before they were born.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 says that 'against one target it is decisive': the picture of death as 'a condition they will find themselves in: a darkness, a silence, an interminable wait'.

Why not (A): Pain in dying is never discussed; the argument concerns non-existence after death, not the process of dying.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says that against this fear 'the symmetry breaks down'; it is the fear the argument fails to remove.

Why not (D): The passage says the time before birth 'did not trouble you then and does not trouble you now'; there is no such regret for the argument to address.

Common misconception: Picking the fear of missing future goods, which the passage discusses at greater length.

Difficulty: Paragraph 2 names the target explicitly. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-052-Q2 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
The final sentence of paragraph 3, contrasting being born earlier with dying later, is used to support the claim that
  1. (A)the fear of death as a dark and silent condition is a confusion that the mirror argument exposes.
  2. (B)the goods lost by dying would have belonged to this very life, whereas the goods of earlier centuries could never have been ours.

CAT-VARC-P01-052-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's objection to the symmetry argument in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)Many people who say they fear death admit, when questioned closely, that they picture it as a dark and silent condition they will endure.
  2. (B)Epicurus and Lucretius both wrote in the hope of freeing their readers from fears they considered groundless.
  3. (C)Some people who are dying report that they fear the process of dying far more than they fear being dead.
  4. (D)On reflection, people regret the goods they missed by being born late as much as the goods they will lose by dying.

If reflective people in fact regretted the goods they missed by being born late just as much, our attitudes would be symmetrical after all, and the mirror would apply to the fear of loss too. The author's further point about whose goods are at stake would still need answering, but this is the only option that bears on the objection at all; the others concern fears the author has already conceded or set aside.

CAT-VARC-P01-052-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The last sentence of the passage implies that
  1. (A)any argument that wholly removed the fear of losing one's future would also weaken a concern that the author regards as legitimate.
  2. (B)no argument about death can offer any genuine consolation, since every fear of death reflects care for the future.

CAT-VARC-P01-053 · Traditions and the good life · passage Medium · 437 words · 4 questions

On the eve of a battle, the Bhagavad Gita relates, the warrior Arjuna loses heart at the prospect of fighting his own kinsmen, and his charioteer Krishna answers him in a long discourse. One of its central teachings is that a person has a claim on his action but not on its fruits: he should act, and act well, without making the outcome the motive of what he does, and he should meet success and failure with the same evenness of mind. The teaching has been admired across many centuries and many traditions, and it has also been accused of something close to moral recklessness. An agent who does not care about outcomes, the objection runs, is an agent who will not count the cost of what he does.

The objection rests on running together two different things: attending to consequences and depending on them. Nothing in the idea of acting without attachment forbids a person from considering what his action is likely to bring about; a physician who acts in this spirit still chooses the treatment most likely to help. What the teaching asks is that the physician's steadiness, and his sense of having done what he should, not be held hostage to whether the patient recovers. Consequences enter into deciding what to do. They do not enter into the agent's claim to a reward, or into the anxious craving that so often bends judgement toward whatever promises to be noticed and praised.

Seen this way, detachment is less a withdrawal from the world than a condition for acting well in it. Anxiety about results is notoriously bad for performance; the craftsman watching the clock and the speaker imagining the applause both tend to do worse work than the one absorbed in the task. More seriously, an agent fixed on fruits is tempted to choose the action whose success will be visible over the one that is right, and to abandon a just course the moment it begins to fail. The teaching guards against both distortions.

It would be too quick, however, to declare the tension dissolved. Consequences cannot be considered in choosing and then set aside entirely in acting, because most actions unfold over time and must be adjusted as their effects appear. The physician has to watch whether the treatment is working. What the teaching can sensibly ask is not blindness to results but a particular relation to them, in which they are information to be used rather than verdicts on one's worth. Whether that relation can be sustained by ordinary people under real pressure is a question the teaching poses rather than settles.

CAT-VARC-P01-053-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
It can be inferred that those who accuse the teaching of 'moral recklessness' understand it to recommend
  1. (A)meeting success with pride and failure with an equally strong sense of shame.
  2. (B)refusing to act at all whenever the outcome of an action is uncertain.
  3. (C)acting without weighing what one's actions are likely to bring about.
  4. (D)choosing whatever action will be most visibly successful and most widely praised.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 states the objection: 'An agent who does not care about outcomes ... is an agent who will not count the cost of what he does.' The critics therefore read the teaching as recommending action without weighing consequences, which paragraph 2 says confuses 'attending to consequences' with 'depending on them'.

Why not (A): The teaching asks for 'the same evenness of mind' in success and failure; neither the teaching nor the objection concerns pride and shame.

Why not (B): The objection concerns an agent who acts but 'will not count the cost'; it does not say the teaching recommends inaction, and the teaching itself says one 'should act'.

Why not (D): This is the distortion paragraph 3 says the teaching guards against, not what critics take it to recommend.

Common misconception: Confusing the critics' reading with the teaching's actual demand for evenness of mind.

Difficulty: The critics' interpretation is stated in the sentence after the accusation. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-053-Q2 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following teachers best shows the relation to results that paragraph 4 says the teaching can sensibly ask for?
  1. (A)One who gives up teaching a difficult class altogether once her first method fails, concluding from the failure that she simply lacks the talent for the work.
  2. (B)One who keeps using a method she believes is right without checking whether her pupils are learning anything from it.
  3. (C)One who chooses whichever methods will produce the most impressive results for visiting inspectors, whether or not her pupils actually benefit from them.
  4. (D)One who sees that a new method is not helping her class, changes it, and does not take the failure as proof she is a poor teacher.

Answer: (D)

Why not (B): This is the 'blindness to results' that paragraph 4 says the teaching does not ask for; the physician 'has to watch whether the treatment is working'.

Why not (C): This is the agent 'fixed on fruits' of paragraph 3, choosing 'the action whose success will be visible over the one that is right'.

CAT-VARC-P01-053-Q3 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
Paragraph 3 contributes to the argument chiefly by
  1. (A)restating the objection of paragraph 1 in a stronger form before the author's final reply in paragraph 4.
  2. (B)going beyond showing that detachment is compatible with good action to claim that it actually helps action go well.
  3. (C)conceding that anxiety about results is sometimes useful because it keeps agents attentive to what they do.
  4. (D)introducing the difficulty that most actions unfold over time and so must be adjusted as their effects gradually appear.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 shows only that the teaching does not forbid attending to consequences. Paragraph 3 then argues that detachment is 'a condition for acting well', because anxiety harms performance and fixation on fruits tempts agents toward visible rather than right action. It moves from compatibility to positive benefit.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 does not restate the objection; it continues the defence begun in paragraph 2 by naming benefits of detachment.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says anxiety about results is 'notoriously bad for performance'; it makes no such concession.

Why not (D): That difficulty is raised in paragraph 4, which begins 'It would be too quick, however'; paragraph 3 is still part of the defence.

Common misconception: Assigning paragraph 4's qualification to paragraph 3.

Difficulty: Requires seeing how paragraph 3 advances beyond paragraph 2, not merely what it says. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-054 · Traditions and the good life · passage Medium · 406 words · 4 questions

Public discussion tends to treat time alone as a problem to be solved. Loneliness is described, rightly, as a source of real suffering, and the remedies proposed, from neighbourhood programmes to ever more ingenious ways of connecting online, share an assumption: that the less time a person spends unaccompanied, the better. On this view solitude and loneliness are a single condition seen from two angles, the first merely a polite name for the second.

The assumption confuses a fact about circumstances with a fact about experience. Loneliness is the painful sense that one's connections are fewer or thinner than one needs; it can be felt in a crowded room, and some of its most persistent forms occur within marriages and among colleagues who see each other every day. Solitude is simply being alone, and its quality depends on what one brings to it. For someone who is lonely, an empty evening confirms a lack. For someone who is not, the same evening may be the only time in the week when attention is not divided, when a thought can be followed to its end, or when grief or pleasure can be felt without being performed for anyone.

Many traditions have valued this second kind of aloneness. Hermits, monks and contemplatives have sought it deliberately, sometimes for years at a time, and periods of withdrawal into wilderness or retreat recur in the stories told about religious teachers in very different cultures. One need not share their aims to see the point they grasped: a person who can never bear his own company depends on others in a way that makes his relationships less free. Friendship is richer between people who could survive without it.

This is not an argument for withdrawal. Solitude is good largely because it is chosen and because it comes to an end; a solitude imposed by exclusion, or one that stretches on with no prospect of return, turns into the very loneliness from which it was supposed to be distinct. Nor is the capacity to be alone a substitute for company; it is a companion skill to it. But if the two conditions really are distinct, then policies and technologies that aim only at reducing time spent alone may, without anyone intending it, crowd out a good that most people need in some measure and few are ever taught to use. The cure for loneliness is better connection, not the abolition of solitude.

  2. (B)is always chosen by the person who suffers it, while solitude is always imposed by others.
  3. (C)is a circumstance of being physically alone, whereas solitude is a feeling about the state of one's relationships with others.
  4. (D)arises only when a person lives alone for a long period with no prospect of company returning.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 defines loneliness as 'the painful sense that one's connections are fewer or thinner than one needs' and says it 'can be felt in a crowded room', while 'Solitude is simply being alone'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says solitude is good 'because it is chosen', and nothing says loneliness is chosen; the option reverses and overstates.

Why not (C): This reverses the passage: solitude 'is simply being alone', a circumstance, and loneliness is 'the painful sense' of thin connections.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says loneliness 'can be felt in a crowded room' and within marriages, so it does not arise only from living alone.

Common misconception: Reversing which term names the circumstance and which names the experience.

Difficulty: The definitions are given directly in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-055 · Traditions and the good life · passage Difficult · 600 words · 5 questions

In ordinary speech leisure is what is left over when work is done. It is time off, and its value is usually explained by reference to the work it interrupts: we rest so that we can return refreshed, and an employer who grants generous holidays is said to be investing in productivity. Even our defences of free time tend to be framed in these terms. Leisure, on this picture, is the pause that makes the next effort possible, and a person who takes too much of it is quietly suspected of having failed at something.

Aristotle saw the relation the other way round. In the Nicomachean Ethics he argues that we are busy in order to have leisure, much as we make war in order to have peace, and the Greek word for leisure, schole, is the ancestor of our word 'school'. Leisure in his sense was not rest or idleness. It was the condition of being free to engage in activities chosen for their own sake, of which he held contemplation to be the highest, though he also counted the cultivated enjoyment of music among its proper occupations. Amusement, by contrast, belonged with work. We need relaxation because we cannot labour continuously, and amusement provides it, so amusement exists for the sake of further activity rather than as an end in itself; to toil for the sake of amusement struck him as childish.

The modern inversion has a consequence that is easy to miss. Once leisure is understood as recovery for the sake of work, it inherits the standards of work. Free time becomes something to be used well, which increasingly means used productively: hobbies are pursued for the skills they build, exercise for the stamina it sustains, even sleep for its effect on the next day's performance. The result is not merely that people have too little time off, though many do, but that the time they have is colonised by the logic it was supposed to interrupt. Aristotle would have recognised the pattern. A life organised entirely around work and the recovery it requires has, in his terms, no leisure at all, however many holidays it contains.

The obvious objection is that Aristotle's leisure was a privilege resting on the labour of others. The citizens free for philosophy and politics were supported by slaves, by women whose sphere he confined to the household, and by labourers and artisans whom he considered unsuited to full citizenship in the best city. A conception of the good life that excludes most people from it can hardly serve as a model for a society in which nearly everyone must work. This objection is just, and it should not be softened. But it tells against the distribution of leisure in Aristotle's city, not against his account of what leisure is. One can reject the arrangement that gave a few men freedom from necessity while still holding that freedom from necessity is valuable for what it makes possible, and that what it makes possible is not simply more work.

What follows for us is less a programme than a question. If the value of leisure lies in activities worth doing for their own sake, then the measure of a society's free time is not only how much of it there is but whether people have the means, the education and the settled habits to fill it with such activities, rather than with recovery alone. That is a demand on schools and cities as much as on employers, and it is one that the language of rest and productivity has no words to state.

  2. (B)provides the relaxation that we need because we cannot labour without a break, and so serves further activity.
  3. (C)was available only to citizens who were supported by the labour of slaves and artisans.
  4. (D)is an activity chosen for its own sake and is therefore the highest use of a person's free time.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: 'We need relaxation because we cannot labour continuously, and amusement provides it, so amusement exists for the sake of further activity rather than as an end in itself'. That is why it belongs with work.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says this of leisure, not amusement, and it is not the reason given for classing amusement with work.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the opposite: amusement exists 'for the sake of further activity rather than as an end in itself', and contemplation is the highest activity.

Common misconception: Assuming that amusement, as a pleasant free-time activity, must be what Aristotle meant by leisure.

Difficulty: The reason is stated in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-056 · Traditions and the good life · passage Difficult · 593 words · 5 questions

Few doctrines have been more often misunderstood by sympathetic outsiders than the Buddhist teaching of anatta, usually translated as 'no-self'. Stated baldly, as the claim that there is no self, it seems to invite an easy refutation: someone, after all, is entertaining the doctrine, and someone will be disappointed if it turns out to be false. Critics who press this point imagine that they have found a contradiction at the heart of the tradition. In fact they have mostly found a problem of translation, and behind it a mistaken assumption about what kind of claim the teaching was meant to be.

In the early discourses the teaching typically appears not as a thesis about what exists but as an exercise. The listener is led through the components that make up a person, which the tradition groups as bodily form, feeling, perception, mental formations and consciousness, and is asked of each whether it is permanent, whether it is reliably satisfying and whether it is under one's control. Since none of them passes these tests, none, the discourse concludes, should be regarded as 'mine', as what 'I am', or as 'my self'. The point of the exercise is practical. According to the tradition's own account of suffering, its origin lies in craving, and craving is sustained by the habit of treating changing and uncontrollable processes as possessions one must defend. To see that nothing in experience is fit to be owned in that way is to loosen the grip of the habit.

Read in this light, the easy refutation misses its target. The teaching does not deny that there is a stream of experiences, nor that the stream has a history, makes choices and bears their consequences; the same tradition has a great deal to say about all three. What it denies is that there is, in addition to the stream, an unchanging owner standing behind it, and it denies this chiefly because belief in such an owner is what makes us cling. A later dialogue makes the point with a chariot: the word names an assembly of axle, wheels and frame, and nothing is lost by admitting that there is no further chariot over and above the parts, arranged as they are. The person, likewise, is a convenient name for a functioning whole, not a hidden thing to which the whole belongs.

It would be too convenient, however, to conclude that the doctrine is purely therapeutic and therefore immune to philosophical scrutiny. A therapy that works by inducing a belief earns its authority only if the belief is warranted; otherwise it is a comforting fiction, and the tradition has never presented its teaching as a fiction. The exercise succeeds, by its own standards, because it reveals how things are. So the metaphysical question cannot be set aside; it can only be stated more precisely. The question is not whether anyone exists but whether a person is anything more than the organised stream, and on that question the doctrine takes a position that some philosophers in other traditions have also defended, and others have rejected, without anyone having settled the matter.

The lesson for the sympathetic outsider is therefore double. The easy refutation fails, because it attacks a claim the teaching does not make. But the easy defence fails too, because the teaching is not merely a technique; it is a technique whose authority rests on a claim about the self that may be true or false, and that deserves to be argued about on its merits rather than admired from a respectful distance.

  3. (C)existed before the person's birth and whether it will continue after the person dies.
  4. (D)is a cause of suffering, whether it can be removed and whether it is a form of craving.
Answer and explanation

Why not (B): None of these tests appears; paragraph 2 lists permanence, reliable satisfaction and control.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 asks whether each component 'is permanent', not about its existence before birth or after death.

Why not (D): Suffering and craving appear later in paragraph 2 as the purpose of the exercise, not as the questions asked of each component.

Common misconception: Picking the option that uses the tradition's vocabulary of suffering and craving.

Difficulty: The three tests are listed in one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P01-056-Q5 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
The chariot example in paragraph 3 serves to
  1. (A)show that the tradition regards persons as fictions, since the word 'chariot' names nothing that really exists in the world.
  2. (B)illustrate how a name can pick out an organised whole without there being any further thing over and above its parts.
  3. (C)prove that the components of a person are permanent, since the parts of a chariot survive when it is taken apart.
  4. (D)settle the metaphysical question that paragraph 4 raises about whether a person is more than an organised stream.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The example comes right after the claim that the teaching denies only an owner 'in addition to the stream'. The word 'chariot' names an 'assembly of axle, wheels and frame', and the person is likewise 'a convenient name for a functioning whole, not a hidden thing to which the whole belongs'.

Why not (A): The example says 'nothing is lost' by admitting there is no further chariot; the assembly is real, and the person is a 'functioning whole', not a fiction.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says none of the components is permanent, and the example says nothing about what survives disassembly.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says this question has not been settled; the example illustrates the doctrine's position without proving it.

Common misconception: Reading the example as saying that persons, like chariots, do not exist at all.

Difficulty: The function is signalled by 'likewise', but one wrong option turns the conventional name into a denial of existence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET
